The Friend Who Stays is an essay by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins). A friendship that endures through the years, tested by distance and time.

An original Signature essay, written for this database in 2026.

The friend who stays is the friend who shows up, again and again, without being asked; staying is the true test of friendship. The essay builds from small scenes, a phone call, a visit, a letter, to a general claim about loyalty. As evidence, the essay says: "Doing nothing, and staying, is the thing itself."

Word count: 2017 words in 135 sentences across 1 paragraphs. Average sentence length: 2017 / 135 = 14.9 words. Reading time: 2017 / 200 = 10.09, rounded up to 11 minutes. Longest word: 'nineteenyearold' (15 letters). Unique words: 602.

Written by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins) as an original essay for this database.

Original Signature work — property of Justin Addam Higgins.

Original Signature essay, written for this database in 2026. No third-party source; no real-author attribution.

Full text

There is a kind of friendship that makes no announcement of itself. It does not arrive with speeches or ceremonies. It grows the way a tree grows beside a house, slowly and without permission, until one day you look out of the window and realise that its shade has become part of how you live. Most of us can count such friends on one hand, and often on fewer fingers than that. They are not the friends of the loud years, the ones who filled the room and then vanished when the room emptied. They are the ones who stayed when there was nothing in it for them to stay for. I first understood this distinction in the winter I lost my job. It was not a dramatic loss. There was no scandal and no shouting. The firm simply closed its doors on a Friday, and on Monday there was nowhere to go. For the first week I treated it as a holiday. I slept late, read the newspaper from cover to cover, and told myself that something better would come along. By the third week the holiday had curdled. I stopped answering the telephone. I stopped shaving. I developed the particular talent of the unemployed, which is the ability to make a small task fill an entire day, and then to feel exhausted by it. It was during those weeks that my address book sorted itself. The friends I had collected in the years of plenty fell away with a speed that would have been comic if it had not been so instructive. Some sent a single message of sympathy and then disappeared, as if sympathy were a debt that could be settled in one payment. Some offered help that was designed to be refused: a vague invitation to "let me know if there's anything I can do," delivered in a tone that made it clear that the correct answer was "nothing, thank you." One or two simply stopped calling, and I understood, without resentment, that our friendship had been a friendship of circumstances, and the circumstances had changed. And then there was Daniel. Daniel had been my friend since we were nineteen, which is to say that he had known me before I had anything worth envying and before I had anything worth losing. He did not send a message. He came to the house on a Tuesday morning, let himself in with the key I had given him years before, and put the kettle on. He did not ask how I was feeling. He asked whether I had eaten. When I said I had not, he made toast and put too much butter on it, the way he always had, and we sat at the kitchen table and talked about football, which neither of us follows with any real attention, and about the roof, which needed repairing, and about nothing at all. He stayed for two hours. He came again on Thursday. He came the following Tuesday. He never once mentioned the job. What Daniel understood, and what I only learned much later, is that the friend who stays does not arrive with solutions. Solutions are cheap. Anyone can suggest a solution from a safe distance. The friend who stays arrives with presence, which is expensive, because it costs time and attention and the willingness to sit in a room where nothing is going well. Presence is the whole of it. The rest is decoration. I have thought about this a great deal since, because I have had the chance to be on both sides of the arrangement. Years later, when Daniel's mother died, I discovered how difficult presence actually is. I wanted to say something wise. I wanted to find the sentence that would make the grief smaller. There is no such sentence. I drove to his house and sat with him, and said very little, and felt useless the entire time, and stayed anyway. Afterwards he told me it was the only visit that had helped, which surprised me, because I had done nothing at all. But that was the point. Doing nothing, and staying, is the thing itself. We are taught to value friendship by its pleasures: the laughter, the shared jokes, the easy evenings. Those are real, and they matter. But they are not the test. The test of a friendship is what it does in the lean season, when there is nothing to celebrate and nothing to gain. A friendship that only works in good weather is not a friendship; it is an alliance of convenience, and there is nothing wrong with alliances of convenience, provided we do not mistake them for something else. The mistake is costly, because it leaves us unprepared for the day when we need a friend and discover that we have only acquaintances. The friend who stays is also, I have noticed, the friend who tells the truth. Not the brutal truth, which is often just brutality wearing a good name, but the plain truth, delivered at the right time and in the right tone. Daniel once told me, in the middle of that unemployed winter, that I was feeling sorry for myself, and that I had been feeling sorry for myself for three weeks, and that it was time to stop. He said it while washing the dishes, without looking at me, in the same voice he used to discuss the weather. It stung, and then it helped, and I have never forgotten that the two things can be true at once. A friend who will not tell you an uncomfortable truth is a friend who has decided that your comfort matters more than your life, and that is not kindness. It is cowardice with good manners. There is another quality I have come to watch for, which is memory. The friend who stays remembers who you were before you became whoever you are now. Daniel remembers the nineteen-year-old who was afraid of telephones and who once got lost on the way to his own interview. When I am tempted to believe my own reputation, Daniel's memory is a corrective. When I am tempted to believe my own failures are the whole story, his memory is a corrective in the other direction. He holds the long version of me, the version that stretches back before the job and the house and the various successes and embarrassments, and he holds it without effort, the way you hold your own name. I do not think such friendships can be made on purpose. They can be tended, once they exist, but they cannot be manufactured. They are the product of time and shared weather. Two people go through enough seasons together, and something grows between them that neither of them planted. The best we can do is to recognise it when it appears, and to treat it as the rare thing it is. If there is a practical lesson in all of this, it is a simple one: be the friend who stays. Answer the telephone. Go to the house. Put the kettle on. Do not wait to be asked, because the person who needs you will very likely not ask. Do not bring solutions unless you are asked for them. Bring yourself, and bring time, and bring the willingness to sit in an uncomfortable room. It is a small thing, and it is almost everything. I still think of that winter sometimes, not with pain, but with a kind of gratitude. It taught me which of my friendships were real, and it taught me what real friendship asks of the people in it. It asks for presence. It asks for honesty. It asks for memory. And it asks, above all, that when the weather changes, you do not leave. I have also learned that the friend who stays is not always the friend you expected. When I was young I imagined friendship as a matter of choosing the right people, as if it were a skill, like choosing the right wine. I kept a mental list of qualities: clever, amusing, well-connected, interesting. I collected people the way other people collect stamps, and I was proud of the collection. It took me years to understand that the qualities that make a person good company at dinner are not the qualities that make a person good company in trouble. The amusing friend is not always the staying friend. The interesting friend is not always the reliable one. The friend who stays is often the quiet one, the one you overlooked at the party, the one who never performed friendship for an audience but simply practised it, day after day, without applause. There is a story my grandmother used to tell about two farmers who lived on either side of a river. Every spring the river flooded, and every spring each farmer would watch the water rise and wonder whether the other would come to help. One year the flood was worse than usual, and the first farmer crossed the river in a small boat, though the current was strong, and helped the second farmer move his animals to higher ground. The second farmer never forgot it, and for the rest of their lives they were friends. My grandmother always ended the story the same way: "He did not cross the river because he was brave. He crossed it because he was a neighbour." I did not understand the story when I was young. I thought it was about courage. It is about something plainer than courage. It is about showing up. Showing up is the most underrated act in human life. We treat it as the minimum, as the thing you do before the real work begins. But in friendship, showing up is the real work. The telephone call, the visit, the kettle, the two hours at the kitchen table: these are not the preliminaries to friendship. They are friendship itself. Everything else we add on top, the advice and the gifts and the grand gestures, is secondary. A grand gesture is easy. It takes an afternoon and a credit card. Showing up takes months and years, and it takes them in small, unremarkable instalments that nobody applauds. I think about this now whenever someone asks me how to make friends as an adult, which is a question I hear often, usually from people who have moved to a new city or lost a partner or retired from a long career. They want a method. They want a set of steps. I have no method to offer them, only this: find one person, and stay. Stay through the boring parts. Stay through the parts that are not about you. Stay when there is nothing to gain. The rest takes care of itself, not quickly, and not always, but more often than you would think. Friendship is not a search. It is a practice. You do not find the friend who stays. You become the friend who stays, and in time, you discover who stayed beside you. If I could leave one thought with anyone reading this, it would be the thought that friendship is not measured in years or in words but in returns. Count the returns. Count the times a person came back when there was no reason to come back. That is the number that matters. Everything else is weather. The friend who stays is the person who keeps coming back, through the ordinary weeks and the hard ones, carrying nothing but themselves and their time. And if you are lucky enough to have such a friend, tell them. Do not assume they know. People who stay rarely hear that their staying mattered, because staying looks so quiet from the outside. Say it plainly, while you can, and then stay beside them in return. Anyone can stand beside you in the sunshine. The friend who stays stands beside you in the rain, without an umbrella, and without complaint, and waits with you until it passes. That is the whole of friendship, and it is enough.

They are friendship itself. Everything else we add on top, the advice and the gifts and the grand gestures, is secondary. A grand gesture is easy. It takes an afternoon and a credit card. Showing up takes months and years, and it takes them in small, unremarkable instalments that nobody applauds.\nI think about this now whenever someone asks me how to make friends as an adult, which is a question I hear often, usually from people who have moved to a new city or lost a partner or retired from a long career. They want a method. They want a set of steps. I have no method to offer them, only this: find one person, and stay. Stay through the boring parts. Stay through the parts that are not about you. Stay when there is nothing to gain. The rest takes care of itself, not quickly, and not always, but more often than you would think. Friendship is not a search. It is a practice. You do not find the friend who stays. You become the friend who stays, and in time, you discover who stayed beside you.\nIf I could leave one thought with anyone reading this, it would be the thought that friendship is not measured in years or in words but in returns. Count the returns. Count the times a person came back when there was no reason to come back. That is the number that matters. Everything else is weather. The friend who stays is the person who keeps coming back, through the ordinary weeks and the hard ones, carrying nothing but themselves and their time. And if you are lucky enough to have such a friend, tell them. Do not assume they know. People who stay rarely hear that their staying mattered, because staying looks so quiet from the outside. Say it plainly, while you can, and then stay beside them in return.\nAnyone can stand beside you in the sunshine. The friend who stays stands beside you in the rain, without an umbrella, and without complaint, and waits with you until it passes. That is the whole of friendship, and it is enough.'

A Garden Made by Hand is an essay by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins). Making a garden by hand, slowly, over years, and what the making teaches.

An original Signature essay, written for this database in 2026.

A garden made by hand is a record of attention; the work is the point, not the result. Concrete detail, soil, seed, and sun, carries the reflection, and the garden becomes the argument. As evidence, the essay says: "In the garden, acceleration is a form of damage."

Word count: 2068 words in 136 sentences across 1 paragraphs. Average sentence length: 2068 / 136 = 15.2 words. Reading time: 2068 / 200 = 10.34, rounded up to 11 minutes. Longest word: 'misunderstand' (13 letters). Unique words: 636.

Written by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins) as an original essay for this database.

Full text

The garden behind my house was, for the first ten years I lived here, a rectangle of neglect. It was not a wilderness in the romantic sense. It was a flat, tired piece of ground where the grass grew in patches, where a broken fence leaned at an angle that suggested it had given up, and where the previous owner had left a rusted barbecue, a cracked plastic chair, and a pile of bricks that may once have had a purpose. I looked at it through the kitchen window every morning and thought, one day. One day is the most durable of all human plans. It survives for years without ever being executed. The day finally came in the spring of the year my daughter left home. The house was suddenly quiet in a way that made the silence audible. I found myself walking from room to room with nothing to do, and the garden, which had waited a decade, finally got its hearing. I went outside with a spade I had owned for years and never used, and I put it into the ground, and the ground, which was mostly clay, resisted. That resistance was the beginning of the garden, and also the beginning of my education. I knew nothing about gardening. I knew the names of perhaps a dozen plants, and half of those I knew only from packets of seeds in the supermarket. I did not know my soil. I did not know my aspect, which is a word I learned that first week and have used with quiet pride ever since. I did not know the difference between a perennial and an annual, except in the vaguest sense. What I had, instead of knowledge, was time, and stubbornness, and a willingness to be wrong in public, since the garden is visible to the neighbours, and every mistake I made was on display. I began, as most beginners do, by doing too much. I dug up the whole rectangle in a single weekend, which left me with a bad back and a large area of bare earth that the rain turned to mud within a week. I bought plants because I liked their pictures, without asking whether they liked clay, or shade, or the particular corner of the world where I live. I planted a rose in the darkest corner and a fern in the sunniest, and both died with a promptness that felt personal. I watered everything every day, whether it needed it or not, and I was surprised when the roots rotted. A garden, I learned, does not reward enthusiasm. It rewards attention. Attention is a different thing from enthusiasm. Enthusiasm is loud and fast. Attention is quiet and slow. I began to pay attention. I started to notice where the sun fell at different hours, and which parts of the ground dried first after rain, and where the wind came from, and which corner the frost settled in. I kept a notebook, though I was embarrassed by it at first, as if keeping notes about a garden were an admission of something. The notebook became the most valuable tool I owned. In it I wrote down what I planted, where I planted it, when I planted it, and what happened. Over the years the notebook filled with small, unglamorous facts: the beans did well on the south side; the slugs ate the hostas; the lavender survived the winter but the rosemary did not; the blackbirds took the strawberries before I did, and there was nothing to be done about it. The blackbirds taught me the second lesson, which is that a garden is not yours. You may own the land, and you may pay the taxes, and you may do the digging, but the garden belongs to everything that lives in it, and your share of it is negotiated daily. The bees arrive whether you invite them or not. The worms do most of the real work underground, unobserved and unpaid. The birds eat your fruit and repay you in song, which is not a fair exchange by any honest accounting, but it is the exchange on offer, and you learn to accept it. I stopped thinking of the garden as a possession and started thinking of it as a household, one in which I was the newest member and the least qualified. There is a particular pleasure in work that cannot be hurried. The garden taught me that pleasure, because it refused every attempt I made to hurry it. You cannot hurry a seed. You cannot hurry a tree. You can dig faster, and water more, and buy larger plants, but the growth happens at its own speed, and your impatience is irrelevant to it. This was hard for me to accept, because most of my working life had been organised around deadlines and acceleration. In the garden, acceleration is a form of damage. The plants that I rushed died. The plants that I left alone, and watched, and waited for, lived. I began to understand why old gardeners move slowly. It is not that they are tired. It is that they have learned the speed of the work. The work itself changed me in ways I did not expect. Digging clay in the rain is not pleasant, and I will not pretend otherwise. But there is a satisfaction in it that is difficult to describe without sounding sentimental. It is the satisfaction of doing something with your hands that has a visible result. You dig a bed, and the bed is there. You plant a row, and the row is there. At the end of the day you can stand at the kitchen window and see what you did. So much of modern work leaves nothing to look at. You answer messages, and the messages are gone. You attend meetings, and the meetings evaporate. The garden leaves evidence. That evidence mattered to me more than I had expected, especially in the years when the house was quiet. People ask me, sometimes, what the garden is for. They mean: do you grow food, or is it ornamental, or is it for the children? I never know how to answer, because the garden is not for anything in the sense they mean. It is not a project with an output. It is a place where I spend time, and the time I spend there is the point. Yes, I grow beans and courgettes and more tomatoes than any household can eat, and I give the surplus away, and that is a pleasure. Yes, there are flowers, and they are beautiful, and their beauty is not wasted on me. But the real crop of the garden is the hours themselves: the mornings with the watering can, the evenings with the secateurs, the long, slow afternoons of weeding, during which my mind does the quiet sorting that it never manages indoors. Weeding deserves a word of its own, because weeding is the work that nobody photographs. It is slow, repetitive, and unglamorous, and it is most of what gardening actually is. You kneel on the ground and pull up the things you did not plant, one by one, and you do it again the following week, because they come back. I used to resent weeding. I thought of it as the price of the garden, the tax I paid for the flowers. Over time I came to think of it differently. Weeding is the garden's way of teaching you that maintenance is the work, not the interruption of the work. The garden does not get finished and then stay finished. It gets tended, or it goes back to being a rectangle of neglect. Most good things are like this, I think. A friendship, a marriage, a skill, a house: none of them are finished. They are tended, or they decline. The garden taught me to stop waiting for the finished state and to find the satisfaction in the tending itself. There were failures, and I should record them honestly, because a garden made by hand is mostly a record of failures with a few successes in between. I killed a fig tree by planting it in the wettest corner. I killed a whole row of carrots by sowing them too deep. I built a compost heap that produced nothing but smell for a year, until a neighbour took pity on me and explained, with great patience, that I had been adding the wrong things in the wrong proportions. I planted mint in open ground, which is a mistake that every gardener makes exactly once, because mint, once established, behaves like an invading army, and I spent two years digging it out. Each failure taught me something, but only if I was willing to admit it was a failure. The garden is a strict teacher. It does not accept excuses. If the plant died, something was wrong, and the something was usually me. The neighbours watched all of this, as neighbours do. At first they watched with the polite silence of people who do not wish to interfere. Then, gradually, they began to offer advice, and plants, and cuttings, and, on one memorable occasion, a wheelbarrow. The garden became a small economy of gifts. Mrs. Patel gave me raspberry canes. Mr. Okafor gave me a rose cutting and three hours of instruction on how to prune it. I gave away courgettes to everyone on the street, including people I had never spoken to before. The garden made me a neighbour in a way that ten years of living in the house had not. I had thought of gardening as a solitary activity. It turned out to be a social one, conducted over fences and across driveways, in the language of cuttings and surplus and shared weather. It is now a good garden, or good enough. The fence stands straight. The grass grows, mostly, where it should. There are beds that produce food, and borders that produce flowers, and a small apple tree that produces more apples than I can use, which is the definition of a successful apple tree. There is a bench under the tree, and on fine evenings I sit on it with a cup of tea and look at what my hands have made, and I feel something close to peace. Not triumph. Peace. The garden did not make me a different person. It made me a slower one, and a more attentive one, and that was enough. The seasons have their own lessons, and the garden teaches them in order. Spring is the season of hope, when everything you plant might live, and the whole garden is a set of promises. It is the easiest season to love, and the easiest to misunderstand, because spring makes gardening look simple. Summer is the season of abundance and of work, when the watering must be done every day and the weeds grow faster than the plants, and you learn whether your spring promises were honest. Autumn is the season of reckoning, when you see what actually grew, and you put the garden to bed, and you make your notes for next year. Winter is the season of patience, when the garden looks like nothing at all, and the real work is the waiting. I used to think winter was the garden's dead season. I now think it is the garden's honest season, the one that shows you the shape of the place without decoration. A gardener who loves only spring does not love the garden. He loves the idea of it. If you have a rectangle of neglect behind your house, and a spade you have never used, I would say this: begin. Begin badly. Begin in ignorance. Plant the rose in the wrong place and watch it die, and learn from it, and plant another. Keep a notebook. Pay attention to where the sun falls. Accept the blackbirds. Do the weeding. Do not wait for the day when you know enough, because that day never comes, and the garden will wait for you longer than you think, but not for ever. A garden made by hand is not a display. It is a conversation, carried on over years, between a person and a piece of ground. And like any good conversation, it repays the time you give it, slowly, and in full.

# Program: full analysis of A Garden Made by Hand
TEXT = "The garden behind my house was, for the first ten years I lived here, a rectangle of neglect. It was not a wilderness in the romantic sense. It was a flat, tired piece of ground where the grass grew in patches, where a broken fence leaned at an angle that suggested it had given up, and where the previous owner had left a rusted barbecue, a cracked plastic chair, and a pile of bricks that may once have had a purpose. Yes, I grow beans and courgettes and more tomatoes than any household can eat, and I give the surplus away, and that is a pleasure. Yes, there are flowers, and they are beautiful, and their beauty is not wasted on me. But the real crop of the garden is the hours themselves: the mornings with the watering can, the evenings with the secateurs, the long, slow afternoons of weeding, during which my mind does the quiet sorting that it never manages indoors.\nWeeding deserves a word of its own, because weeding is the work that nobody photographs. It is slow, repetitive, and unglamorous, and it is most of what gardening actually is. You kneel on the ground and pull up the things you did not plant, one by one, and you do it again the following week, because they come back. I used to resent weeding. I thought of it as the price of the garden, the tax I paid for the flowers. Over time I came to think of it differently. Weeding is the garden's way of teaching you that maintenance is the work, not the interruption of the work. The garden does not get finished and then stay finished. It gets tended, or it goes back to being a rectangle of neglect. Most good things are like this, I think. A friendship, a marriage, a skill, a house: none of them are finished. They are tended, or they decline. The garden taught me to stop waiting for the finished state and to find the satisfaction in the tending itself.\nThere were failures, and I should record them honestly, because a garden made by hand is mostly a record of failures with a few successes in between. I killed a fig tree by planting it in the wettest corner. I killed a whole row of carrots by sowing them too deep. I built a compost heap that produced nothing but smell for a year, until a neighbour took pity on me and explained, with great patience, that I had been adding the wrong things in the wrong proportions. I planted mint in open ground, which is a mistake that every gardener makes exactly once, because mint, once established, behaves like an invading army, and I spent two years digging it out. Each failure taught me something, but only if I was willing to admit it was a failure. The garden is a strict teacher. It does not accept excuses. If the plant died, something was wrong, and the something was usually me.\nThe neighbours watched all of this, as neighbours do. At first they watched with the polite silence of people who do not wish to interfere. Then, gradually, they began to offer advice, and plants, and cuttings, and, on one memorable occasion, a wheelbarrow. The garden became a small economy of gifts. Mrs. Patel gave me raspberry canes. Mr. Okafor gave me a rose cutting and three hours of instruction on how to prune it. I gave away courgettes to everyone on the street, including people I had never spoken to before. The garden made me a neighbour in a way that ten years of living in the house had not. I had thought of gardening as a solitary activity. It turned out to be a social one, conducted over fences and across driveways, in the language of cuttings and surplus and shared weather.\nIt is now a good garden, or good enough. The fence stands straight. The grass grows, mostly, where it should. There are beds that produce food, and borders that produce flowers, and a small apple tree that produces more apples than I can use, which is the definition of a successful apple tree. There is a bench under the tree, and on fine evenings I sit on it with a cup of tea and look at what my hands have made, and I feel something close to peace. Not triumph. Peace. The garden did not make me a different person. It made me a slower one, and a more attentive one, and that was enough.\nThe seasons have their own lessons, and the garden teaches them in order. Spring is the season of hope, when everything you plant might live, and the whole garden is a set of promises. It is the easiest season to love, and the easiest to misunderstand, because spring makes gardening look simple. Summer is the season of abundance and of work, when the watering must be done every day and the weeds grow faster than the plants, and you learn whether your spring promises were honest. Autumn is the season of reckoning, when you see what actually grew, and you put the garden to bed, and you make your notes for next year. Winter is the season of patience, when the garden looks like nothing at all, and the real work is the waiting. I used to think winter was the garden's dead season. I now think it is the garden's honest season, the one that shows you the shape of the place without decoration. A gardener who loves only spring does not love the garden. He loves the idea of it.\nIf you have a rectangle of neglect behind your house, and a spade you have never used, I would say this: begin. Begin badly. Begin in ignorance. Plant the rose in the wrong place and watch it die, and learn from it, and plant another. Keep a notebook. Pay attention to where the sun falls. Accept the blackbirds. Do the weeding. Do not wait for the day when you know enough, because that day never comes, and the garden will wait for you longer than you think, but not for ever. A garden made by hand is not a display. It is a conversation, carried on over years, between a person and a piece of ground. And like any good conversation, it repays the time you give it, slowly, and in full."

The City at First Light is an essay by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins). The city in the early morning, before it wakes, and what the quiet reveals.

An original Signature essay, written for this database in 2026.

The city at first light belongs to almost no one, and the few who are there see it truly. The essay is a walk, hour by hour, from dark to dawn, and the walking is the thinking. As evidence, the essay says: "At dawn it is empty except for the stallholders setting up, and the pigeons, and the smell of the night's rain on the cobbles."

Full text

There is an hour in every city that belongs to almost no one. It comes before the traffic and after the night, in the blue interval when the street lights are still on but no longer needed, and the sky is deciding what kind of day it will be. I discovered this hour by accident, in the year I worked the early shift at the bakery, and I have been returning to it ever since, not because I must, but because I choose to. It is the best hour of the day, and it is free, and almost nobody takes it. I walk the same route most mornings, though the route changes with the seasons and with my mood. I leave the house when it is still dark, and I walk to the river, and then along the river to the market square, and then home again by the long way, past the station and the church and the row of shops that have not yet opened. The whole walk takes an hour. In that hour I see the city the way it actually is, before it puts on its daytime face. The first thing you notice, walking a city before dawn, is the quiet. It is not silence. There is always sound: a distant train, a lorry on the ring road, the hum of a refrigerator vent behind a restaurant. But the human noise is gone, and in its absence the city sounds like a machine idling, or like a house in which everyone is still asleep. Your own footsteps are loud in it. You become aware of your own breathing. It is a strange and pleasant feeling, to be the only moving thing in a place built for millions. The second thing you notice is the people who are already up, and who they are. The early city belongs to a particular population, and it is not the population you see at noon. There are the bakers, of course, and the cleaners, and the men who collect the rubbish, and the women who open the flower stalls. There are the nurses coming off the night shift, walking home with the particular tiredness that comes from twelve hours of other people's emergencies. There are the taxi drivers waiting for fares that have not yet arrived, drinking coffee from paper cups and talking in low voices. There are the runners, who pass you in the half-light like ghosts, and the dog walkers, and the one old man who feeds the pigeons in the square at exactly the same time every morning, whatever the weather. These are the citizens of the first light, and they recognise each other, the way people do who share a secret. I have come to know some of them by sight, and a few by name. There is Elena, who runs the bakery where I used to work, and who still waves me in for a roll when the door is open, though I no longer work there. There is Mr. Adeyemi, who drives the first bus of the day, and who nods at me every morning with the gravity of a man performing a ceremony. There is the woman with the red coat who walks her greyhound along the river, and who has never spoken to me, and probably never will, and with whom I have nevertheless exchanged a thousand nods of mutual recognition. These are not friendships. They are something smaller and, in their way, just as valuable: the daily acknowledgement that we are both here, both awake, both part of the same early world. The city itself looks different at this hour, and not only because the light is different. The buildings, which by day are backdrops to the business of living, become the main event. You notice the brickwork. You notice the windows, dark or lit, and you wonder about the lives behind them. You notice the details that the daytime crowd walks past without seeing: the carved stone above a doorway, the faded sign of a shop that closed thirty years ago, the small garden someone has made in a window box, the mural on the side of the warehouse that nobody looks at because everyone is looking at their phones. The city at first light is a museum with no admission charge and no other visitors. I am particularly fond of the market square at dawn. By day it is crowded and loud, full of stalls and shoppers and arguments about the price of fish. At dawn it is empty except for the stallholders setting up, and the pigeons, and the smell of the night's rain on the cobbles. The stallholders work with a practised economy of movement, unfolding awnings, stacking crates, arranging their goods in the particular order that each of them believes is correct. I have watched the flower seller arrange her buckets for years, and I still cannot tell you her system, but there is a system, and it matters to her, and that is enough. There is dignity in work done well before anyone is watching, and the market square at dawn is full of it. As the light grows, the city begins to change, and I have learned to watch for the moment of the change. It is not a single moment. It comes in stages. First the sky lightens at the edges. Then the birds begin, one at a time, and then all at once, as if someone had given a signal. Then the first commuters appear, walking fast, looking at their watches, carrying coffee, already rehearsing the day. Then the shutters go up, one by one, with a rattle that echoes down the street. Then the traffic begins, and the noise rises, and the quiet hour is over, and the city belongs to everyone again. I am usually on my way home by then, and I feel, absurdly, as if I have had something that the rest of the day will not give back. People ask me why I get up so early, when I no longer have to. They assume there must be a reason: insomnia, or anxiety, or a fitness regime. The truth is simpler. I get up early because the early hour is the only hour of the day that is entirely mine. The rest of the day is divided up and claimed by other people, by obligations, by messages, by the thousand small demands that arrive from the moment the telephone starts ringing. But the first hour belongs to me. Nobody expects anything of me at five in the morning. Nobody sends me a message at five in the morning that cannot wait. It is the one hour in which I am not performing a role for anyone, not employee, not parent, not neighbour, not friend. I am only a person walking through a city, looking at things, and that is enough. I have also found that the early walk orders the rest of the day. On the mornings when I walk, I am calmer, and kinder, and more patient than on the mornings when I do not. I do not know why this is. Perhaps it is the exercise. Perhaps it is the quiet. Perhaps it is the simple fact of having begun the day by doing something I chose, rather than something that was chosen for me. Whatever the reason, the effect is real, and it lasts. A day that begins with an hour of walking begins on my terms, and the rest of the day, even when it goes badly, goes badly on top of a good beginning. There are practical things I have learned from these walks, too. I have learned the weather in a way that no forecast can teach. I can tell, from the colour of the sky over the river, whether it will rain before noon. I can tell, from the way the wind moves through the square, whether it will be a cold day or a mild one. I have learned the city's rhythms: which streets flood in heavy rain, which cafes open earliest, which corners catch the sun first in winter. This knowledge is useless in any formal sense. It will not earn me money or advancement. But it is mine, and it makes the city feel like home in a way that no amount of living in it ever did before I started walking it. I sometimes think about the people who will never see the city at first light, and I do not blame them. Sleep is a good thing, and a warm bed is a good thing, and there is no moral virtue in being awake before dawn. But I think they are missing something, and I think the something they are missing is not the beauty of the sunrise, though the sunrise over the river is beautiful, but the feeling of having a city to yourself, even briefly. There is a particular kind of freedom in being the only person on a street that will, in two hours, be full of thousands. It is the freedom of the empty theatre before the audience arrives, of the kitchen before the cooks arrive. It is a small freedom, and it costs nothing, and it is available every morning to anyone willing to set an alarm. The city at first light has taught me to pay attention, which is the same lesson the garden taught me, and I suspect it is the same lesson that most good things teach, if you stay with them long enough. Attention is the rarest commodity in modern life, and the early morning is one of the few places where it is still freely available. There is nothing competing for it at five in the morning. No advertisements. No notifications. No noise. Just the city, and the light, and the sound of your own footsteps, and the slow, daily miracle of a place waking up. The walk has also changed how I see the people of the daytime city. By day, the streets are full of strangers, and strangers are easy to ignore. But after you have seen the same streets empty, and watched them fill, you start to notice the individuals inside the crowd. The woman who always carries two bags. The man who reads as he walks, and never once looks up, and never once collides with anything, which I have never understood. The schoolchildren in their uniforms, loud in the morning and silent in the afternoon. They were always there. I simply never looked at them before the early walks taught me how to look. Attention, once practised, does not switch itself off at seven in the morning. It stays with you for the rest of the day, and the day is better for it. There is one more thing the early city has given me, and it is the least expected of all: a sense of belonging that I did not have before. I have lived in this city for thirty years. I have paid its taxes and voted in its elections and shopped in its shops. But I did not feel that it was mine until I started walking it before dawn. There is something about being present when a place is at its most private that makes you feel like a part of it rather than a user of it. The city at first light does not perform for you. It does not advertise itself. It simply is, and if you are there to see it, you are part of it for an hour. That is a small thing, and it is not small at all. I will keep walking. I will keep setting the alarm, and putting on my coat, and stepping out into the blue interval between night and day. The city will keep waking up without me, whether I am there to see it or not. But I will be there to see it, as often as I can, because the hour belongs to almost no one, and I have decided that it belongs, for a little while each morning, to me.

# Program: full analysis of The City at First Light
TEXT = "There is an hour in every city that belongs to almost no one. It comes before the traffic and after the night, in the blue interval when the street lights are still on but no longer needed, and the sky is deciding what kind of day it will be. I discovered this hour by accident, in the year I worked the early shift at the bakery, and I have been returning to it ever since, not because I must, but because I choose to. These are not friendships. They are something smaller and, in their way, just as valuable: the daily acknowledgement that we are both here, both awake, both part of the same early world.\nThe city itself looks different at this hour, and not only because the light is different. The buildings, which by day are backdrops to the business of living, become the main event. You notice the brickwork. You notice the windows, dark or lit, and you wonder about the lives behind them. You notice the details that the daytime crowd walks past without seeing: the carved stone above a doorway, the faded sign of a shop that closed thirty years ago, the small garden someone has made in a window box, the mural on the side of the warehouse that nobody looks at because everyone is looking at their phones. The city at first light is a museum with no admission charge and no other visitors.\nI am particularly fond of the market square at dawn. By day it is crowded and loud, full of stalls and shoppers and arguments about the price of fish. At dawn it is empty except for the stallholders setting up, and the pigeons, and the smell of the night's rain on the cobbles. The stallholders work with a practised economy of movement, unfolding awnings, stacking crates, arranging their goods in the particular order that each of them believes is correct. I have watched the flower seller arrange her buckets for years, and I still cannot tell you her system, but there is a system, and it matters to her, and that is enough. There is dignity in work done well before anyone is watching, and the market square at dawn is full of it.\nAs the light grows, the city begins to change, and I have learned to watch for the moment of the change. It is not a single moment. It comes in stages. First the sky lightens at the edges. Then the birds begin, one at a time, and then all at once, as if someone had given a signal. Then the first commuters appear, walking fast, looking at their watches, carrying coffee, already rehearsing the day. Then the shutters go up, one by one, with a rattle that echoes down the street. Then the traffic begins, and the noise rises, and the quiet hour is over, and the city belongs to everyone again. I am usually on my way home by then, and I feel, absurdly, as if I have had something that the rest of the day will not give back.\nPeople ask me why I get up so early, when I no longer have to. They assume there must be a reason: insomnia, or anxiety, or a fitness regime. The truth is simpler. I get up early because the early hour is the only hour of the day that is entirely mine. The rest of the day is divided up and claimed by other people, by obligations, by messages, by the thousand small demands that arrive from the moment the telephone starts ringing. But the first hour belongs to me. Nobody expects anything of me at five in the morning. Nobody sends me a message at five in the morning that cannot wait. It is the one hour in which I am not performing a role for anyone, not employee, not parent, not neighbour, not friend. I am only a person walking through a city, looking at things, and that is enough.\nI have also found that the early walk orders the rest of the day. On the mornings when I walk, I am calmer, and kinder, and more patient than on the mornings when I do not. I do not know why this is. Perhaps it is the exercise. Perhaps it is the quiet. Perhaps it is the simple fact of having begun the day by doing something I chose, rather than something that was chosen for me. Whatever the reason, the effect is real, and it lasts. A day that begins with an hour of walking begins on my terms, and the rest of the day, even when it goes badly, goes badly on top of a good beginning.\nThere are practical things I have learned from these walks, too. I have learned the weather in a way that no forecast can teach. I can tell, from the colour of the sky over the river, whether it will rain before noon. I can tell, from the way the wind moves through the square, whether it will be a cold day or a mild one. I have learned the city's rhythms: which streets flood in heavy rain, which cafes open earliest, which corners catch the sun first in winter. This knowledge is useless in any formal sense. It will not earn me money or advancement. But it is mine, and it makes the city feel like home in a way that no amount of living in it ever did before I started walking it.\nI sometimes think about the people who will never see the city at first light, and I do not blame them. Sleep is a good thing, and a warm bed is a good thing, and there is no moral virtue in being awake before dawn. But I think they are missing something, and I think the something they are missing is not the beauty of the sunrise, though the sunrise over the river is beautiful, but the feeling of having a city to yourself, even briefly. There is a particular kind of freedom in being the only person on a street that will, in two hours, be full of thousands. It is the freedom of the empty theatre before the audience arrives, of the kitchen before the cooks arrive. It is a small freedom, and it costs nothing, and it is available every morning to anyone willing to set an alarm.\nThe city at first light has taught me to pay attention, which is the same lesson the garden taught me, and I suspect it is the same lesson that most good things teach, if you stay with them long enough. Attention is the rarest commodity in modern life, and the early morning is one of the few places where it is still freely available. There is nothing competing for it at five in the morning. No advertisements. No notifications. No noise. Just the city, and the light, and the sound of your own footsteps, and the slow, daily miracle of a place waking up.\nThe walk has also changed how I see the people of the daytime city. By day, the streets are full of strangers, and strangers are easy to ignore. But after you have seen the same streets empty, and watched them fill, you start to notice the individuals inside the crowd. The woman who always carries two bags. The man who reads as he walks, and never once looks up, and never once collides with anything, which I have never understood. The schoolchildren in their uniforms, loud in the morning and silent in the afternoon. They were always there. I simply never looked at them before the early walks taught me how to look. Attention, once practised, does not switch itself off at seven in the morning. It stays with you for the rest of the day, and the day is better for it.\nThere is one more thing the early city has given me, and it is the least expected of all: a sense of belonging that I did not have before. I have lived in this city for thirty years. I have paid its taxes and voted in its elections and shopped in its shops. But I did not feel that it was mine until I started walking it before dawn. There is something about being present when a place is at its most private that makes you feel like a part of it rather than a user of it. The city at first light does not perform for you. It does not advertise itself. It simply is, and if you are there to see it, you are part of it for an hour. That is a small thing, and it is not small at all.\nI will keep walking. I will keep setting the alarm, and putting on my coat, and stepping out into the blue interval between night and day. The city will keep waking up without me, whether I am there to see it or not. But I will be there to see it, as often as I can, because the hour belongs to almost no one, and I have decided that it belongs, for a little while each morning, to me."

On Keeping a Notebook is an essay by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins). The habit of keeping a notebook, and how it preserves a life.

An original Signature essay, written for this database in 2026.

A notebook is a second memory; writing things down is how a life is kept. The essay moves between the notebook's contents and the life they record, and each illuminates the other. As evidence, the essay says: "When you write something down by hand, you have to choose it."

Full text

I have kept a notebook for most of my adult life, and I have almost nothing to show for it. That is the first thing to understand about notebooks. They are not investments. They do not pay dividends. They do not, in my experience, produce books, or fortunes, or reputations. What they produce is something quieter and, I have come to believe, more valuable: a record of having paid attention. The notebook is where attention goes to live after the moment has passed. My first notebook was a cheap exercise book with a blue cover, bought in the year I left school. I filled it with the kind of things a nineteen-year-old thinks are worth writing down: lines from songs, arguments I wished I had won, the names of girls who did not know I existed, and long, solemn paragraphs about the state of the world, written in a hand that slanted hopefully towards the future. I lost that notebook somewhere between two rented rooms, and I mourned it for a week, and then I bought another one, and then another. I have been buying them ever since, at a rate of perhaps two a year, and I now have a shelf full of them, in various states of decay, their pages filled with a handwriting that changes with the decades. People who do not keep notebooks often ask what goes into them, as if there were a correct answer. There is no correct answer. There is only your answer. Mine contains, on any given page, a mixture of the following: things I have seen, things I have heard, things I have read, things I have thought, things I need to remember, and things I am afraid of forgetting. The proportions change from year to year. In my twenties the notebook was mostly thoughts, and the thoughts were mostly about myself. In my thirties it was mostly lists, because I had a young family and a job and a mortgage, and the lists were a way of keeping the days from falling apart. In my forties it became mostly observations, because I had begun to notice that the world was more interesting than my opinions about it. Now, in my sixties, it is mostly a record of small things: the first snowdrop, the price of bread, a sentence from a conversation, the way the light fell across the kitchen table on a particular Tuesday. The notebook has taught me that memory is not a filing cabinet. It is not a place where experiences are stored, intact, waiting to be retrieved. Memory is a storyteller, and a dishonest one. It edits. It flatters. It forgets the parts that do not suit the story it wants to tell. I have learned this by going back through old notebooks and finding entries that contradict what I remember. I remember a holiday as happy; the notebook records that I was miserable for most of it, and that the happiness came only on the last day. I remember a quarrel as being entirely the other person's fault; the notebook records, in my own handwriting, that I started it. I remember a decision as careful and considered; the notebook shows that I made it in an afternoon, on impulse, and spent the next year pretending it had been a plan. The notebook does not lie. It cannot lie, because it was written before I knew how the story would end. This is the notebook's first gift: it keeps you honest. Not honest in the grand, moral sense, but honest in the small, daily sense of knowing what actually happened, as opposed to what you later decided happened. That kind of honesty is rarer than it sounds, and more useful. Most of us live inside a story about ourselves that we have edited so many times we no longer remember what was in the first draft. The notebook is the first draft. It is unedited, and unflattering, and true, and it waits patiently on the shelf until you are ready to read it. The notebook's second gift is that it slows you down. Writing by hand is slow. It is slower than thinking, and much slower than typing. That slowness is not a defect. It is the point. When you write something down by hand, you have to choose it. You cannot write down everything, so you must decide what matters. That decision is a form of attention, and attention is the scarcest thing in a life full of noise. I have sat on trains and watched people scroll through their telephones, consuming hundreds of images and messages in a few minutes, and I have wondered what any of it will mean to them in a year. The notebook asks the opposite question: of all the things that happened today, which one is worth a page? The act of choosing is the act of valuing, and the valuing is the point. There is also the matter of the hand itself. I write with a fountain pen, not out of snobbery, but because a fountain pen will not let you rush. It demands a certain pace, and a certain pressure, and if you hurry it, it lets you know. A ballpoint will forgive anything. A fountain pen will not. I like that about it. I like tools that have opinions. My notebooks, too, have opinions. They are particular about paper. They dislike cheap paper, which bleeds, and they prefer paper with a little tooth to it, paper that holds the ink rather than letting it slide. These are small preferences, and they sound absurd when I write them down, but they are part of the pleasure. A notebook is a physical thing, and its physical qualities matter. The weight of it in your hand. The sound of the page turning. The smell of the paper. These are not incidental. They are part of why the notebook works, because it engages the body as well as the mind, and a memory that involves the body lasts longer than one that does not. I should say something about what the notebook is not. It is not a diary, in the sense of a daily record of events. I do not write in it every day, and I have never tried to. A diary is a duty, and duties, in my experience, are the quickest way to kill a habit. The notebook is not a duty. It is an invitation. I write in it when I have something to write, and I leave it alone when I do not. Some weeks I fill ten pages. Some months I fill none. The notebook does not mind. It waits. That patience is part of its character, and part of why I have kept the habit for forty years while other habits, adopted with much more enthusiasm, lasted a fortnight. It is also not a journal in the therapeutic sense, though it has, at times, served that purpose. I have written pages in it that no one will ever read, pages of anger and grief and confusion, and the writing of them helped, not because the notebook offered advice, but because it offered a place to put things down. There is a relief in putting a thought on paper, in seeing it outside your own head, where it is smaller and less frightening than it was inside. I do not think the notebook heals anything. I think it gives you a place to set things down while you work out what to do with them. That is enough. The question I am asked most often about notebooks is whether I ever go back and read them. The answer is yes, but not in the way people imagine. I do not sit down and read a notebook from beginning to end, the way you would read a book. I dip into them. I open one at random and read a page, and I am transported, not to the past as it was, but to the past as I recorded it, which is a different country. Sometimes the entries make me laugh. Sometimes they make me wince. Sometimes they make me wonder who that person was, writing so earnestly about things that no longer matter, in a handwriting I barely recognise as my own. But always, underneath the laughter and the wincing, there is a feeling of gratitude: gratitude to the younger version of myself who took the trouble to write it down, so that I could remember what I would otherwise have forgotten. There is a practical side to all of this, and I should not neglect it. The notebook is useful. It holds telephone numbers and addresses and recipes and measurements and the names of plants and the dates of appointments and the titles of books I mean to read. It holds ideas for work, and fragments of conversations, and quotations that struck me, and questions I have not yet answered. I have solved problems in my notebook that I could not solve in my head, because the act of writing them down forced me to state them clearly, and a clearly stated problem is often halfway solved. I have also lost things in my notebook, whole pages of notes that I can no longer find, because I did not date them or index them, and I have learned, slowly, to be more careful about that. A notebook is a tool, and like any tool it works better when you look after it. But usefulness is not the reason I keep one. I keep one because it is the most reliable way I know of paying attention to my own life. Life passes quickly, and most of it passes unnoticed. The days blur together. The years blur together. Without a record, you wake up one morning and discover that a decade has gone by and you cannot say what happened in it. The notebook is my defence against that. It is not a perfect defence. It records only a fraction of what happens, and the fraction it records is chosen by a person who is not always a reliable judge of what matters. But a fraction is better than nothing, and the act of choosing is itself a way of living more deliberately. There is one more thing the notebook has given me, and it is the most unexpected of all: it has made me a better listener. When you know you might write something down, you listen differently. You listen for the sentence worth keeping, for the detail that will matter later, for the thing that was said almost in passing and would otherwise be lost. I have caught myself, in conversations, listening with a pencil in my mind, waiting for the line that deserves a page. That sounds calculating, and perhaps it is, a little. But the effect is that I pay closer attention to the people I am with, and they can tell. People know when they are being listened to, and they know when they are not. The notebook, oddly enough, has made me more present, not less, because it gives my attention somewhere to go. If you have never kept a notebook, I would suggest starting with a cheap one. Do not buy an expensive leather-bound volume, because an expensive notebook is intimidating, and an intimidating notebook stays empty. Buy something cheap, something you will not be afraid to spoil. Write in it badly. Write in it messily. Cross things out. Make mistakes. The notebook does not care. It only asks that you show up, now and then, with a pen, and put down something true. That is all. And if you keep doing it, year after year, you will find, as I have, that you have built something without noticing: a record of a life, written in your own hand, in real time, before you knew how it would turn out. There are few possessions more valuable than that, and none that cost so little.

# Program: full analysis of On Keeping a Notebook
TEXT = "I have kept a notebook for most of my adult life, and I have almost nothing to show for it. That is the first thing to understand about notebooks. They are not investments. They do not pay dividends. They do not, in my experience, produce books, or fortunes, or reputations. What they produce is something quieter and, I have come to believe, more valuable: a record of having paid attention. The notebook is where attention goes to live after the moment has passed.\nMy first notebook was a cheap exercise book with a blue cover, bought in the year I left school. I filled it with the kind of things a nineteen-year-old thinks are worth writing down: lines from songs, arguments I wished I had won, the names of girls who did not know I existed, and long, solemn paragraphs about the state of the world, written in a hand that slanted hopefully towards the future. I lost that notebook somewhere between two rented rooms, and I mourned it for a week, and then I bought another one, and then another. I have been buying them ever since, at a rate of perhaps two a year, and I now have a shelf full of them, in various states of decay, their pages filled with a handwriting that changes with the decades.\nPeople who do not keep notebooks often ask what goes into them, as if there were a correct answer. There is no correct answer. There is only your answer. Mine contains, on any given page, a mixture of the following: things I have seen, things I have heard, things I have read, things I have thought, things I need to remember, and things I am afraid of forgetting. The proportions change from year to year. In my twenties the notebook was mostly thoughts, and the thoughts were mostly about myself. In my thirties it was mostly lists, because I had a young family and a job and a mortgage, and the lists were a way of keeping the days from falling apart. In my forties it became mostly observations, because I had begun to notice that the world was more interesting than my opinions about it. Now, in my sixties, it is mostly a record of small things: the first snowdrop, the price of bread, a sentence from a conversation, the way the light fell across the kitchen table on a particular Tuesday.\nThe notebook has taught me that memory is not a filing cabinet. It is not a place where experiences are stored, intact, waiting to be retrieved. Memory is a storyteller, and a dishonest one. It edits. It flatters. It forgets the parts that do not suit the story it wants to tell. I have learned this by going back through old notebooks and finding entries that contradict what I remember. I remember a holiday as happy; the notebook records that I was miserable for most of it, and that the happiness came only on the last day. I remember a quarrel as being entirely the other person's fault; the notebook records, in my own handwriting, that I started it. I remember a decision as careful and considered; the notebook shows that I made it in an afternoon, on impulse, and spent the next year pretending it had been a plan. The notebook does not lie. It cannot lie, because it was written before I knew how the story would end.\nThis is the notebook's first gift: it keeps you honest. Not honest in the grand, moral sense, but honest in the small, daily sense of knowing what actually happened, as opposed to what you later decided happened. That kind of honesty is rarer than it sounds, and more useful. Most of us live inside a story about ourselves that we have edited so many times we no longer remember what was in the first draft. The notebook is the first draft. It is unedited, and unflattering, and true, and it waits patiently on the shelf until you are ready to read it.\nThe notebook's second gift is that it slows you down. Writing by hand is slow. It is slower than thinking, and much slower than typing. That slowness is not a defect. It is the point. When you write something down by hand, you have to choose it. You cannot write down everything, so you must decide what matters. That decision is a form of attention, and attention is the scarcest thing in a life full of noise. I have sat on trains and watched people scroll through their telephones, consuming hundreds of images and messages in a few minutes, and I have wondered what any of it will mean to them in a year. The notebook asks the opposite question: of all the things that happened today, which one is worth a page? The act of choosing is the act of valuing, and the valuing is the point.\nThere is also the matter of the hand itself. I write with a fountain pen, not out of snobbery, but because a fountain pen will not let you rush. It demands a certain pace, and a certain pressure, and if you hurry it, it lets you know. A ballpoint will forgive anything. A fountain pen will not. I like that about it. I like tools that have opinions. My notebooks, too, have opinions. They are particular about paper. They dislike cheap paper, which bleeds, and they prefer paper with a little tooth to it, paper that holds the ink rather than letting it slide. These are small preferences, and they sound absurd when I write them down, but they are part of the pleasure. A notebook is a physical thing, and its physical qualities matter. The weight of it in your hand. The sound of the page turning. The smell of the paper. These are not incidental. They are part of why the notebook works, because it engages the body as well as the mind, and a memory that involves the body lasts longer than one that does not.\nI should say something about what the notebook is not. It is not a diary, in the sense of a daily record of events. I do not write in it every day, and I have never tried to. A diary is a duty, and duties, in my experience, are the quickest way to kill a habit. The notebook is not a duty. It is an invitation. I write in it when I have something to write, and I leave it alone when I do not. Some weeks I fill ten pages. Some months I fill none. The notebook does not mind. It waits. That patience is part of its character, and part of why I have kept the habit for forty years while other habits, adopted with much more enthusiasm, lasted a fortnight.\nIt is also not a journal in the therapeutic sense, though it has, at times, served that purpose. I have written pages in it that no one will ever read, pages of anger and grief and confusion, and the writing of them helped, not because the notebook offered advice, but because it offered a place to put things down. There is a relief in putting a thought on paper, in seeing it outside your own head, where it is smaller and less frightening than it was inside. I do not think the notebook heals anything. I think it gives you a place to set things down while you work out what to do with them. That is enough.\nThe question I am asked most often about notebooks is whether I ever go back and read them. The answer is yes, but not in the way people imagine. I do not sit down and read a notebook from beginning to end, the way you would read a book. I dip into them. I open one at random and read a page, and I am transported, not to the past as it was, but to the past as I recorded it, which is a different country. Sometimes the entries make me laugh. Sometimes they make me wince. Sometimes they make me wonder who that person was, writing so earnestly about things that no longer matter, in a handwriting I barely recognise as my own. But always, underneath the laughter and the wincing, there is a feeling of gratitude: gratitude to the younger version of myself who took the trouble to write it down, so that I could remember what I would otherwise have forgotten.\nThere is a practical side to all of this, and I should not neglect it. The notebook is useful. It holds telephone numbers and addresses and recipes and measurements and the names of plants and the dates of appointments and the titles of books I mean to read. It holds ideas for work, and fragments of conversations, and quotations that struck me, and questions I have not yet answered. I have solved problems in my notebook that I could not solve in my head, because the act of writing them down forced me to state them clearly, and a clearly stated problem is often halfway solved. I have also lost things in my notebook, whole pages of notes that I can no longer find, because I did not date them or index them, and I have learned, slowly, to be more careful about that. A notebook is a tool, and like any tool it works better when you look after it.\nBut usefulness is not the reason I keep one. I keep one because it is the most reliable way I know of paying attention to my own life. Life passes quickly, and most of it passes unnoticed. The days blur together. The years blur together. Without a record, you wake up one morning and discover that a decade has gone by and you cannot say what happened in it. The notebook is my defence against that. It is not a perfect defence. It records only a fraction of what happens, and the fraction it records is chosen by a person who is not always a reliable judge of what matters. But a fraction is better than nothing, and the act of choosing is itself a way of living more deliberately.\nThere is one more thing the notebook has given me, and it is the most unexpected of all: it has made me a better listener. When you know you might write something down, you listen differently. You listen for the sentence worth keeping, for the detail that will matter later, for the thing that was said almost in passing and would otherwise be lost. I have caught myself, in conversations, listening with a pencil in my mind, waiting for the line that deserves a page. That sounds calculating, and perhaps it is, a little. But the effect is that I pay closer attention to the people I am with, and they can tell. People know when they are being listened to, and they know when they are not. The notebook, oddly enough, has made me more present, not less, because it gives my attention somewhere to go.\nIf you have never kept a notebook, I would suggest starting with a cheap one. Do not buy an expensive leather-bound volume, because an expensive notebook is intimidating, and an intimidating notebook stays empty. Buy something cheap, something you will not be afraid to spoil. Write in it badly. Write in it messily. Cross things out. Make mistakes. The notebook does not care. It only asks that you show up, now and then, with a pen, and put down something true. That is all. And if you keep doing it, year after year, you will find, as I have, that you have built something without noticing: a record of a life, written in your own hand, in real time, before you knew how it would turn out. There are few possessions more valuable than that, and none that cost so little."

The Art of Walking Slowly is an essay by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins). Walking slowly as a remedy for hurry, and for a bad knee.

An original Signature essay, written for this database in 2026.

Walking slowly is not a lesser way of walking; it is a better way of seeing. The essay's pace matches its subject; it strolls, and the strolling is the argument. As evidence, the essay says: "When you walk slowly, your breathing settles, your shoulders drop, and your mind, which has been clenched like a fist all day, begins to open."
