  • Reading time calculated: 11 minutes at 200 words per minute
  • Cover and description supplied
  • Original Signature work; no third-party rights involved

Full text

There is a kind of patience that can only be learned from wood. I learned it from Tom, my teacher, though he never called it patience, and never gave a lesson in it. He simply practised it, day after day, in the workshop, and I watched him, and slowly, without noticing, I began to practise it too. It is a patience that has nothing to do with waiting, and everything to do with attention. It is the patience of a person who knows that the work will take as long as it takes, and who is willing to give it that long. Tom was seventy when I met him, and he had been working with wood for fifty years. He moved slowly in the workshop, not because he was old, though he was, but because he had learned that speed is the enemy of good work. He measured twice, and cut once, and he said it so often that it became a joke among the apprentices, and then it became a habit, and then it became a way of thinking. Measure twice, cut once. It sounds like advice about woodworking, and it is, but it is also advice about everything else. Think twice, speak once. Look twice, decide once. The carpenter's patience is the habit of checking before acting, and of acting only when you are sure. I watched Tom work on a single piece of wood for an entire afternoon, once, and I could not understand what he was doing. He was planing a board, and he planed it, and then he looked at it, and then he planed it again, and then he looked at it again, and the changes he was making were so small that I could not see them. I asked him what he was doing, and he said: "I am getting it flat." I said: "It looks flat." He said: "It is not flat yet." He planed it for another hour, and then he held a straight edge against it, and showed me the gap, a gap so small I could barely see it, and he said: "There. That is what I was working on." I had been watching him for an afternoon, and I had not seen what he was doing, because what he was doing was too small to see, and too important to rush. That is the carpenter's patience: the willingness to work on a gap you can barely see, for an hour, because the gap matters. It matters because the joint will not fit if the board is not flat, and the joint is the foundation of everything that follows. A chair built on a board that is not flat will wobble. A table built on a board that is not flat will rock. The whole structure depends on the small, invisible work that no one will ever see, and the carpenter knows this, and does the work anyway, because the work is the point, not the seeing of it. Patience, in the workshop, is not passive. It is not sitting and waiting for something to happen. It is active. It is the patience of the hand that holds the plane steady, and of the eye that checks the edge, and of the mind that refuses to be hurried. It is a patience that is practised, daily, in small acts, and that becomes, over time, a habit, and then a character. Tom did not become patient by deciding to be patient. He became patient by planing boards, for fifty years, and by learning, board by board, that hurrying made the work worse, and that patience made it better. I have tried to carry the carpenter's patience out of the workshop and into the rest of my life, and I have found that it is harder there, because the rest of my life does not reward patience the way the workshop does. The world outside the workshop rewards speed. It rewards the quick answer, the fast decision, the immediate result. It punishes the person who takes an hour to get a board flat, because the world does not see the board, and does not care whether it is flat. But I care, now, because I have seen what happens when the board is flat, and when it is not, and I have learned that the difference, though invisible, is real, and that it matters, in the end, more than the speed. The carpenter's patience has changed how I read, and how I write, and how I listen. I read more slowly now, because I have learned that a sentence, like a board, needs to be looked at twice before you decide what it means. I write more slowly, because I have learned that a paragraph written in haste will wobble, the way a chair built in haste will wobble. I listen more slowly, because I have learned that a person, like a piece of wood, has a grain, and that if you work against the grain, the person will resist you, and if you work with it, they will help you. These are not metaphors. They are the same patience, applied to different materials. There is a particular moment in woodworking that taught me the most about patience, and it is the moment of the glue drying. When you glue a joint, you clamp it, and then you wait. You cannot use the piece until the glue has set, and the glue sets in its own time, usually overnight. You cannot hurry it. You cannot test it early, because testing it early will break the joint, and you will have to start again. So you wait. You go home. You come back the next day, and you take the clamps off, and you test the joint, gently, and it holds, or it does not. The waiting is the hardest part, because it requires trust: trust in the glue, trust in the joint, trust in the work you did the day before. I have learned to trust the waiting, in the workshop and outside it. Some things need a night to set, and no amount of impatience will make them set faster. Tom retired the year after I finished my first chair, and I have not seen him since, except once, in the town, where we nodded to each other, and he asked me how the chair was holding up, and I told him it leaned slightly to the left, and he laughed, and said: "They all lean a little. That is how you know they were made by hand." I think about that often. The lean is not a defect. It is a signature. It is the mark of the hand that made it, and of the patience that went into it, and of the person who was willing to take the time to make it properly, even if properly meant imperfectly. The carpenter's patience has also taught me about the difference between a job and a craft, which is a distinction I did not understand when I worked in an office. A job is something you do for money, and it is measured by output, and it is finished when the day is over. A craft is something you do for its own sake, and it is measured by quality, and it is never finished, because there is always more to learn. Tom did not have a job. He had a craft. He had spent fifty years learning it, and he was still learning, and he knew it, and that knowledge, that he was still learning, was part of what made him good. A person who thinks they have finished learning has stopped practising their craft, whether they know it or not. I have watched other craftsmen since Tom, in other trades, and I have seen the same patience in all of them: the gardener who waits for the seed, the cook who waits for the dough, the teacher who waits for the student to understand, the parent who waits for the child to grow. Patience is not specific to wood. It is specific to any work that is done well, and done with care, and done over time. The world is full of people practising it, quietly, without recognition, and the world is better for them, though it does not always know it. There is a test for patience, and it is a simple one: can you do the invisible work, and do it well, knowing that no one will ever see it? The board that is flat, the joint that is tight, the glue that has set properly: no one will ever see these things, except the person who made them, and the person who made them knows. That knowing is the reward, and it is enough, if you have learned to value it. If you have not learned to value it, then the invisible work will always feel like a waste of time, and you will always be tempted to skip it, and the work will suffer, and you will not know why. Patience, I have also learned, is contagious, in the way that hurry is contagious. When you are with someone who is patient, you become more patient yourself, without trying. When you are with someone who is hurried, you become hurried, without noticing. Tom's patience spread through the workshop, the way a calm person's calm spreads through a room. The apprentices, watching him, slowed down. They measured twice. They checked their work. They stopped rushing. I have seen the same thing in other places: a teacher who is patient makes patient students, and a parent who is patient makes patient children, and a friend who is patient makes patient friends. Patience is not only a personal quality. It is a gift you give to the people around you, by practising it where they can see it. The opposite is also true, and it is worth saying plainly: hurry is contagious too, and it is more contagious than patience, because the world rewards it, and because it feels like energy, and because it is easier to imitate. I have worked in places where hurry was the culture, and where everyone hurried, and where the hurrying made the work worse, and where no one could see it, because they were all hurrying too fast to look. The workshop was the first place I worked where hurry was not the culture, and where patience was, and the difference was visible in the work. The chairs did not wobble. The joints were tight. The boards were flat. The work was good, and the goodness was the result of the patience, and the patience was the result of a man who had practised it for fifty years, and who had taught it, by example, to everyone who came into his workshop. I have also learned, from the carpenter's patience, that slowness is not the same as laziness, and that confusing them is one of the great mistakes of the modern world. Laziness is the refusal to do the work. Slowness is the refusal to do the work badly. The patient carpenter works slowly because he cares, and because he knows that the work deserves his full attention, and that the attention takes time. The lazy worker does not work at all, or works carelessly, and the carelessness shows. I have been accused of slowness, in my life, and I have learned to accept the accusation, because it is true, and because the slowness is deliberate, and because the work that comes from it is better than the work that comes from hurry. If you are called slow, ask yourself whether the slowness is patience, or laziness, and if it is patience, wear it with pride. I still have the chair. It still leans. It still holds me. And every time I sit on it, I remember Tom, and the afternoon he spent getting a board flat, and the gap so small I could barely see it, and I am reminded that patience is not the absence of action. It is the presence of attention, sustained over time, until the work is done properly. That is the carpenter's patience, and it is the best thing I learned in the workshop, better than the chair, better than the joints, better than the tools. It is a way of being in the world, and I carry it with me, wherever I go.

It is the presence of attention, sustained over time, until the work is done properly. That is the carpenter\'s patience, and it is the best thing I learned in the workshop, better than the chair, better than the joints, better than the tools. It is a way of being in the world, and I carry it with me, wherever I go.'

On Reading Aloud is an essay by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins). Reading aloud, and the difference between the spoken word and the printed one.

An original Signature essay, written for this database in 2026.

Reading aloud is the original form of reading, and still the best. The essay's rhythm is the rhythm of a voice; it is meant to be read aloud, and the reading proves the point. As evidence, the essay says: "The silent reading that we now take for granted is a recent invention, and it is a useful one, but it came at a cost."

Full text

I read aloud to my children when they were small, every night, for years, and I thought, at the time, that I was doing it for them. I was wrong. I was doing it for both of us, and I did not understand that until much later, when the children were grown and the reading aloud had stopped, and I missed it more than I had expected to. Reading aloud is one of the oldest human activities, older than writing, older than books, and it is one of the most neglected. We have forgotten how to do it, and we have forgotten why it matters, and I would like to make the case for remembering. The first thing reading aloud does is slow you down. When you read silently, you read at the speed of your eyes, which is fast, and you skip, and you skim, and you miss things. When you read aloud, you read at the speed of your voice, which is slow, and you cannot skip, and you cannot skim, and you hear every word. The words become physical. They have weight, and rhythm, and sound, and you notice things about them that silent reading never shows you: the alliteration, the repetition, the way a sentence rises and falls, the way a paragraph breathes. A book read aloud is a different book from the same book read silently, and it is, in my experience, a better one. I learned this as a child, when my mother read to me, and I learned it again as a parent, when I read to my children. The books I read aloud to them are the books I remember best, not because the books were better, but because I read them properly, word by word, in a voice, in a room, to people who were listening. I remember the voices I gave the characters, some of which were terrible, and some of which were, by the children's account, exactly right. I remember the questions they asked, which interrupted the story, and which were the best part of it, because they showed that the children were listening, and thinking, and that the story was alive in their minds, not just on the page. Reading aloud is also a social act, in a way that silent reading is not. When you read silently, you are alone, even if you are in a room full of people. When you read aloud, you are together. The reader and the listeners share the story, and the sharing changes it. A funny passage is funnier when it is read aloud, because you hear the laughter of the people listening, and the laughter makes it funnier. A sad passage is sadder, because you hear the silence that follows it, and the silence makes it sadder. A frightening passage is more frightening, because you are in the room together, and the fear is shared. Reading aloud turns a solitary activity into a communal one, and the community is the point. I have read aloud to adults, too, though less often, and I have found that adults need it as much as children do, though they are more embarrassed to admit it. I have read poems aloud to friends, and letters aloud to my wife, and passages from books aloud to anyone who would listen, and I have found that the reading aloud changes the text, and changes the listeners, in ways that silent reading does not. A poem read aloud is a different poem. A letter read aloud is a different letter. The voice gives the words a body, and the body makes them real, in a way that the eye alone cannot. There is a history to reading aloud, and it is worth remembering. For most of human history, reading aloud was the normal way of reading. People read aloud to themselves, and to each other, because books were scarce, and because reading was a public act, and because the ear was the primary organ of literature, not the eye. The silent reading that we now take for granted is a recent invention, and it is a useful one, but it came at a cost. We gained privacy, and speed, and the ability to read anywhere, without disturbing anyone. We lost the sound of the words, and the sharing of the story, and the slow, deliberate pace that reading aloud imposes. I am not suggesting that we should go back to reading everything aloud. I am suggesting that we should remember what we lost, and reclaim some of it. Reading aloud has taught me to choose my words more carefully, in my own writing, because I write with the ear in mind now, and I test my sentences by saying them out loud. A sentence that looks good on the page but sounds wrong in the voice is a bad sentence, and I have learned to hear the difference. I read my own work aloud, before I show it to anyone, and I cross out the sentences that stumble, and I keep the sentences that flow. The voice is a strict editor, stricter than the eye, and it catches things the eye misses: the repetition, the awkwardness, the rhythm that does not work. Every writer should read aloud. Every writer should be read aloud to. It is the oldest test of good writing, and it is still the best. I have also found that reading aloud is a way of paying attention to another person, which is a rare thing in modern life. When you read aloud to someone, you are giving them your time, and your voice, and your attention, and you are asking for theirs in return. It is an intimate act, in the best sense, and it creates a closeness that is difficult to achieve in any other way. I have read aloud to my wife when she was ill, and to my father when he was dying, and to my grandchildren when they were small, and in each case the reading was not about the book. It was about the being together, and the voice, and the story, and the quiet that followed it. There is a particular kind of reading aloud that I value most, and it is the reading of poetry. A poem read silently is a puzzle. A poem read aloud is a song. The rhyme, the metre, the rhythm, the sound of the words against each other: all of these are invisible on the page, and all of them are audible in the voice. I have read poems aloud that I had read silently many times, and I have discovered, in the reading aloud, meanings I had never seen, because the sound revealed them. A line that seemed plain on the page became, in the voice, beautiful. A stanza that seemed obscure became, in the voice, clear. The voice is the poem's natural home, and the page is only a record of it, like a score is a record of a piece of music. You can read a score, and understand it, but you have not heard the music until someone plays it. I have also read aloud in places where reading aloud is not expected, and I have been surprised by the response. I read a passage aloud on a train, once, to a friend who was travelling with me, and the people around us, who had been looking at their telephones, looked up, and listened, and one of them smiled, and another nodded, as if to say: yes, that is a good passage, and I am glad you read it. I do not think they were being polite. I think they were hungry for it, hungry for the sound of words read aloud, in a world where words are mostly silent, and mostly on screens. The hunger is there, I believe, in most people, even if they do not know it. They have forgotten what reading aloud sounds like, and when they hear it, they remember, and they are glad. Reading aloud is also, I have found, a way of remembering. The books I read aloud are the books I remember. The passages I read aloud are the passages I can still quote, years later, word for word, because the saying of them fixed them in my memory in a way that silent reading never did. The voice is a memory aid, and a powerful one. It engages the body, and the ear, and the breath, and the memory that involves the body lasts longer than the memory that involves only the eye. I can still hear, in my mind, the voices I used for the characters in the books I read to my children, and the hearing of them brings back the whole scene: the room, the light, the children, the story. That is a memory worth having, and it was made by reading aloud. Reading aloud has also taught me about the rhythm of a story, which is something that silent reading hides. A story has a rhythm, like a piece of music, and the rhythm is in the sentences: the short ones, and the long ones, and the pauses between them, and the way the pace quickens towards the end of a chapter, and slows at the beginning of the next. When you read silently, you impose your own rhythm on the story, and the story's rhythm is lost. When you read aloud, you must follow the story's rhythm, because the voice cannot do otherwise, and the following of it teaches you how the story works, and why it works, in a way that silent reading never does. I have learned more about writing from reading aloud than I have learned from any book about writing, because reading aloud shows you the rhythm, and the rhythm is the thing that makes a story live. There is a particular pleasure in reading aloud to someone who is falling asleep, which is a pleasure I knew well when my children were small, and which I know again now, with my grandchildren. The voice grows quieter, and slower, and the story becomes a lullaby, and the listener drifts, and the reader goes on, a little longer than necessary, because the reading is itself a comfort, and because the moment, the child half asleep, the room dark, the story nearly finished, is one of the best moments in a life. I have read the same story a hundred times, and I will read it a hundred more, if I am asked, because the story is not the point. The point is the voice, and the listening, and the being together, in the dark, at the end of the day. Reading aloud has also shown me that a voice can be a form of hospitality, which is a thought I have only recently had, and which I like. When you read aloud to someone, you are offering them your voice, and your time, and your attention, and you are making a place for them, in the story, and in the evening, and the offering is a kind of welcome. The reader says, without words: come in, sit down, listen, you are welcome here. The listener says, without words: I am here, I am listening, I am glad to be here. That exchange, simple as it is, is one of the oldest forms of hospitality in the world, and it is still one of the best, and it costs nothing, and it can be offered anywhere, to anyone, at any time. If you have children, read to them. Read to them every night, even when you are tired, even when the book is one you have read a hundred times, even when they could read it themselves. The reading is not about the book. It is about the time together, and the voice, and the story, and the memory they will carry of being read to, which will stay with them long after they have forgotten the story itself. If you do not have children, read to someone else. Read to a friend. Read to a partner. Read to an old person who can no longer read for themselves. Read aloud, and listen to the sound of the words, and notice how different they are from the words on the page. You may find, as I have, that reading aloud is not a lesser form of reading. It is the original form, and it is still the best.

# Program: full analysis of On Reading Aloud
TEXT = "I read aloud to my children when they were small, every night, for years, and I thought, at the time, that I was doing it for them. I was wrong. I was doing it for both of us, and I did not understand that until much later, when the children were grown and the reading aloud had stopped, and I missed it more than I had expected to. They have forgotten what reading aloud sounds like, and when they hear it, they remember, and they are glad.\nReading aloud is also, I have found, a way of remembering. The books I read aloud are the books I remember. The passages I read aloud are the passages I can still quote, years later, word for word, because the saying of them fixed them in my memory in a way that silent reading never did. The voice is a memory aid, and a powerful one. It engages the body, and the ear, and the breath, and the memory that involves the body lasts longer than the memory that involves only the eye. I can still hear, in my mind, the voices I used for the characters in the books I read to my children, and the hearing of them brings back the whole scene: the room, the light, the children, the story. That is a memory worth having, and it was made by reading aloud.\nReading aloud has also taught me about the rhythm of a story, which is something that silent reading hides. A story has a rhythm, like a piece of music, and the rhythm is in the sentences: the short ones, and the long ones, and the pauses between them, and the way the pace quickens towards the end of a chapter, and slows at the beginning of the next. When you read silently, you impose your own rhythm on the story, and the story's rhythm is lost. When you read aloud, you must follow the story's rhythm, because the voice cannot do otherwise, and the following of it teaches you how the story works, and why it works, in a way that silent reading never does. I have learned more about writing from reading aloud than I have learned from any book about writing, because reading aloud shows you the rhythm, and the rhythm is the thing that makes a story live.\nThere is a particular pleasure in reading aloud to someone who is falling asleep, which is a pleasure I knew well when my children were small, and which I know again now, with my grandchildren. The voice grows quieter, and slower, and the story becomes a lullaby, and the listener drifts, and the reader goes on, a little longer than necessary, because the reading is itself a comfort, and because the moment, the child half asleep, the room dark, the story nearly finished, is one of the best moments in a life. I have read the same story a hundred times, and I will read it a hundred more, if I am asked, because the story is not the point. The point is the voice, and the listening, and the being together, in the dark, at the end of the day.\nReading aloud has also shown me that a voice can be a form of hospitality, which is a thought I have only recently had, and which I like. When you read aloud to someone, you are offering them your voice, and your time, and your attention, and you are making a place for them, in the story, and in the evening, and the offering is a kind of welcome. The reader says, without words: come in, sit down, listen, you are welcome here. The listener says, without words: I am here, I am listening, I am glad to be here. That exchange, simple as it is, is one of the oldest forms of hospitality in the world, and it is still one of the best, and it costs nothing, and it can be offered anywhere, to anyone, at any time.\nIf you have children, read to them. Read to them every night, even when you are tired, even when the book is one you have read a hundred times, even when they could read it themselves. The reading is not about the book. It is about the time together, and the voice, and the story, and the memory they will carry of being read to, which will stay with them long after they have forgotten the story itself. If you do not have children, read to someone else. Read to a friend. Read to a partner. Read to an old person who can no longer read for themselves. Read aloud, and listen to the sound of the words, and notice how different they are from the words on the page. You may find, as I have, that reading aloud is not a lesser form of reading. It is the original form, and it is still the best."

The Second Spring is an essay by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins). A second spring in later life: a sunflower, a garden, a new beginning.

An original Signature essay, written for this database in 2026.

Spring comes twice, if you plant a seed, even when you think it is too late. The sunflower is the essay's seed and its proof, and the photograph of it is the essay's memory. As evidence, the essay says: "When you are young, everything seems to matter, and you chase everything, and you exhaust yourself, and you do not know why."

Word count: 2088 words in 111 sentences across 1 paragraphs. Average sentence length: 2088 / 111 = 18.8 words. Reading time: 2088 / 200 = 10.44, rounded up to 11 minutes. Longest word: 'granddaughter' (13 letters). Unique words: 497.

Written by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins) as an original essay for this database.

Full text

There is a spring that comes late in life, and it is not the spring of youth. It is a second spring, and it comes when you least expect it, usually after a winter that felt as if it would never end. I had mine at sixty, in the year after I retired, when I thought my life was settling into its final shape, and I discovered, to my surprise, that it was not settling at all. It was beginning again. The winter before it had been a hard one. I had left my job, and the leaving had been harder than I expected. I had told myself that retirement would be a relief, and in some ways it was. I no longer had to wake to an alarm. I no longer had to sit in meetings. I no longer had to pretend to care about things I did not care about. But I also no longer had a reason to get up, and no one who needed me, and no place where I was expected, and the freedom, which had sounded so appealing, turned out to be a kind of emptiness. I filled the emptiness with tasks, and with television, and with long walks that went nowhere, and I told myself I was content, and I was not content, and I knew I was not content, and I did not know what to do about it. The second spring began, as springs do, with something small. I planted a seed. It was not a metaphor. I literally planted a seed, a sunflower seed, in a pot on the windowsill, because my granddaughter had given it to me, and because she asked me to, and because I had nothing better to do. I planted it, and I watered it, and I forgot about it, and a week later it had sprouted, and I was absurdly pleased, out of all proportion to the event. A seed had sprouted. It was the smallest possible thing, and it changed everything, because it reminded me that growth is still possible, even in a life that feels finished, even in a pot on a windowsill, even at sixty. I began to plant more things. I planted the garden, which I have written about elsewhere, and I began to learn woodworking, which I have also written about, and I began to walk, and to write, and to read, and to do all the things I had put off for years, telling myself I would do them when I had time. I had time now. I had nothing but time. And the time, which had felt like a burden, began to feel like a gift. I was not old, I realised. I was free. The distinction mattered. Old is a condition. Free is an opportunity. The second spring is different from the first, and the difference is worth understanding. The first spring, the spring of youth, is a spring of possibility without knowledge. You do not know what you are doing, and you do not know what will happen, and the not-knowing is exciting, and also frightening. The second spring is a spring of possibility with knowledge. You know what you are doing, more or less, because you have done it before, and you know what can go wrong, and you know how to avoid some of it, and the knowing makes the possibility richer, not poorer. You are not starting from nothing. You are starting from experience, and experience is a better foundation than ignorance, even if it is a heavier one to carry. In the second spring, you also know what matters, in a way that you did not know when you were young. When you are young, everything seems to matter, and you chase everything, and you exhaust yourself, and you do not know why. When you are older, you know that only a few things matter, and you can name them, and you can give your time to them, and let the rest go. I know what matters to me now: my family, my garden, my workshop, my walks, my books, my friends. That is a short list, and it is enough. It is more than enough. It is everything. The second spring has its own work, and the work is different from the work of the first spring. The work of the first spring is to build: to build a career, a family, a house, a life. The work of the second spring is to tend: to tend what you have built, and to tend yourself, and to tend the things that will outlast you. It is quieter work, and slower work, and it does not come with promotions or praise, but it is, I have found, more satisfying. A garden tended is more satisfying than a career built, because the garden is alive, and the career, once built, is only a structure, and structures, however impressive, do not grow. I have met other people in their second spring, and I have recognised them by a certain quality they share: a lightness, a lack of urgency, a willingness to try things without needing to succeed at them. They take up painting, or singing, or languages, or walking, or gardening, and they do it badly, and happily, and they do not care that they do it badly, because the doing is the point, and the point is not the product. I envy them, and I am one of them, and I wish I had known, when I was young, that this lightness was available, and that it did not require waiting until sixty to find it. But perhaps it did require waiting. Perhaps the lightness can only be earned by carrying the heaviness first, and by putting it down, deliberately, when the time comes. There are losses in the second spring, and I should not pretend otherwise. The body is slower. The memory is less reliable. Friends die. The world changes, and not always for the better, and you feel, sometimes, like a stranger in a place you used to know. These losses are real, and they hurt, and they should not be minimised. But they are not the whole story. The whole story includes the gains, and the gains are real too: the time, the freedom, the knowledge, the lightness, the second chance to do the things you did not do the first time, and to do them better, because you know more now. The second spring has also taught me that it is never too late to become the person you wanted to be, though the person you become will be different from the person you imagined. When I was young, I imagined that I would be a person who did great things, and who was known for them, and who left a mark on the world. I am not that person, and I will never be that person, and I have made my peace with it. The person I am is a person who makes chairs that lean, and grows sunflowers in pots, and walks slowly through his town, and writes in a notebook, and I am content with that person, in a way that the young man who wanted to be great never was. The second spring does not give you the life you dreamed of when you were young. It gives you a different life, and the different life, if you are willing to accept it, is enough. There is a freedom in the second spring that I did not have in the first, and it is the freedom of lowered expectations, which sounds like a sad thing, and is not. When you are young, the expectations are high, and they are mostly other people's, and they weigh on you, and you spend your life trying to meet them, and failing, and feeling the failure. When you are older, the expectations fall away, or you put them down, deliberately, and you are left with your own expectations, which are simpler, and kinder, and more honest. I expect of myself now only that I pay attention, and that I am kind, and that I do the work that is in front of me, as well as I can. That is a short list, and I can meet it, most days, and the meeting of it is a satisfaction that the grand ambitions of youth never gave me. The second spring has also changed how I think about time, which is perhaps the most important change of all. When you are young, time feels endless, and you spend it carelessly, as if there were an unlimited supply. When you are older, time feels finite, and you spend it more carefully, and the care makes it more valuable. I do not waste time now, or not as much as I used to, because I know that the supply is limited, and that what I do with it matters. But I also do not hoard time, because hoarding it is another way of wasting it. I use it, deliberately, on the things that matter, and I let the rest go, and the using of it, rather than the saving of it, is the point. I have also found, in the second spring, a new relationship with my own past. When I was young, the past was a burden, a collection of mistakes and missed chances that I carried with me and regretted. Now the past is a resource. It is the material from which the second spring is built. The mistakes taught me what not to do. The missed chances taught me what to do now, while there is still time. The experiences, good and bad, are the foundation, and the foundation is solid, because it was built over a lifetime. I do not regret the past now, or not as much as I did. I use it. That is a better relationship with it, and a kinder one. There are people who never have a second spring, and I think about them often. They retire, and they decline, and they fade, and they tell themselves that their life is over, and they believe it, and the believing makes it true. I do not blame them. The second spring is not guaranteed. It requires a seed, and a willingness to plant it, and a willingness to wait for it to grow, and not everyone has those things, or knows that they have them. But I would say to anyone who thinks their spring is over: it is not over until you decide it is over, and the decision is yours, and you can unmake it, at any time, by planting something, and waiting, and seeing what grows. The second spring has also taught me to be grateful in a way I was not when I was young. When you are young, gratitude feels unnecessary, because everything is ahead of you, and you assume it will always be there. When you are older, and you have a second spring, gratitude becomes natural, because you know that the spring might not have come, and that it did, and that it is a gift, and that gifts should be acknowledged. I am grateful for the sunflower, and for the garden, and for the granddaughter, and for the hands that still work, and for the eyes that still see, and for the mind that still thinks, and for the years that are left, however many there are. Gratitude does not make the second spring last longer. It makes it better, while it lasts. My sunflower grew. It grew tall, and it flowered, and it turned its face to the sun, and my granddaughter was delighted, and I was delighted, and we ate the seeds together, in the autumn, sitting in the garden. It was a small thing, a sunflower in a pot, and it was the beginning of everything that came after. I keep a photograph of it, on the shelf in the quiet room, and I look at it sometimes, and I remember that spring comes twice, if you are lucky, and if you are paying attention, and if you are willing to plant a seed, even when you think it is too late. It is never too late. The seed does not know how old you are. It only knows whether you planted it, and whether you watered it, and whether you waited. Plant it. Water it. Wait. The spring will come.

# Program: full analysis of The Second Spring
TEXT = "There is a spring that comes late in life, and it is not the spring of youth. It is a second spring, and it comes when you least expect it, usually after a winter that felt as if it would never end. When you are young, everything seems to matter, and you chase everything, and you exhaust yourself, and you do not know why. When you are older, you know that only a few things matter, and you can name them, and you can give your time to them, and let the rest go. I know what matters to me now: my family, my garden, my workshop, my walks, my books, my friends. That is a short list, and it is enough. It is more than enough. It is everything.\nThe second spring has its own work, and the work is different from the work of the first spring. The work of the first spring is to build: to build a career, a family, a house, a life. The work of the second spring is to tend: to tend what you have built, and to tend yourself, and to tend the things that will outlast you. It is quieter work, and slower work, and it does not come with promotions or praise, but it is, I have found, more satisfying. A garden tended is more satisfying than a career built, because the garden is alive, and the career, once built, is only a structure, and structures, however impressive, do not grow.\nI have met other people in their second spring, and I have recognised them by a certain quality they share: a lightness, a lack of urgency, a willingness to try things without needing to succeed at them. They take up painting, or singing, or languages, or walking, or gardening, and they do it badly, and happily, and they do not care that they do it badly, because the doing is the point, and the point is not the product. I envy them, and I am one of them, and I wish I had known, when I was young, that this lightness was available, and that it did not require waiting until sixty to find it. But perhaps it did require waiting. Perhaps the lightness can only be earned by carrying the heaviness first, and by putting it down, deliberately, when the time comes.\nThere are losses in the second spring, and I should not pretend otherwise. The body is slower. The memory is less reliable. Friends die. The world changes, and not always for the better, and you feel, sometimes, like a stranger in a place you used to know. These losses are real, and they hurt, and they should not be minimised. But they are not the whole story. The whole story includes the gains, and the gains are real too: the time, the freedom, the knowledge, the lightness, the second chance to do the things you did not do the first time, and to do them better, because you know more now.\nThe second spring has also taught me that it is never too late to become the person you wanted to be, though the person you become will be different from the person you imagined. When I was young, I imagined that I would be a person who did great things, and who was known for them, and who left a mark on the world. I am not that person, and I will never be that person, and I have made my peace with it. The person I am is a person who makes chairs that lean, and grows sunflowers in pots, and walks slowly through his town, and writes in a notebook, and I am content with that person, in a way that the young man who wanted to be great never was. The second spring does not give you the life you dreamed of when you were young. It gives you a different life, and the different life, if you are willing to accept it, is enough.\nThere is a freedom in the second spring that I did not have in the first, and it is the freedom of lowered expectations, which sounds like a sad thing, and is not. When you are young, the expectations are high, and they are mostly other people's, and they weigh on you, and you spend your life trying to meet them, and failing, and feeling the failure. When you are older, the expectations fall away, or you put them down, deliberately, and you are left with your own expectations, which are simpler, and kinder, and more honest. I expect of myself now only that I pay attention, and that I am kind, and that I do the work that is in front of me, as well as I can. That is a short list, and I can meet it, most days, and the meeting of it is a satisfaction that the grand ambitions of youth never gave me.\nThe second spring has also changed how I think about time, which is perhaps the most important change of all. When you are young, time feels endless, and you spend it carelessly, as if there were an unlimited supply. When you are older, time feels finite, and you spend it more carefully, and the care makes it more valuable. I do not waste time now, or not as much as I used to, because I know that the supply is limited, and that what I do with it matters. But I also do not hoard time, because hoarding it is another way of wasting it. I use it, deliberately, on the things that matter, and I let the rest go, and the using of it, rather than the saving of it, is the point.\nI have also found, in the second spring, a new relationship with my own past. When I was young, the past was a burden, a collection of mistakes and missed chances that I carried with me and regretted. Now the past is a resource. It is the material from which the second spring is built. The mistakes taught me what not to do. The missed chances taught me what to do now, while there is still time. The experiences, good and bad, are the foundation, and the foundation is solid, because it was built over a lifetime. I do not regret the past now, or not as much as I did. I use it. That is a better relationship with it, and a kinder one.\nThere are people who never have a second spring, and I think about them often. They retire, and they decline, and they fade, and they tell themselves that their life is over, and they believe it, and the believing makes it true. I do not blame them. The second spring is not guaranteed. It requires a seed, and a willingness to plant it, and a willingness to wait for it to grow, and not everyone has those things, or knows that they have them. But I would say to anyone who thinks their spring is over: it is not over until you decide it is over, and the decision is yours, and you can unmake it, at any time, by planting something, and waiting, and seeing what grows.\nThe second spring has also taught me to be grateful in a way I was not when I was young. When you are young, gratitude feels unnecessary, because everything is ahead of you, and you assume it will always be there. When you are older, and you have a second spring, gratitude becomes natural, because you know that the spring might not have come, and that it did, and that it is a gift, and that gifts should be acknowledged. I am grateful for the sunflower, and for the garden, and for the granddaughter, and for the hands that still work, and for the eyes that still see, and for the mind that still thinks, and for the years that are left, however many there are. Gratitude does not make the second spring last longer. It makes it better, while it lasts.\nMy sunflower grew. It grew tall, and it flowered, and it turned its face to the sun, and my granddaughter was delighted, and I was delighted, and we ate the seeds together, in the autumn, sitting in the garden. It was a small thing, a sunflower in a pot, and it was the beginning of everything that came after. I keep a photograph of it, on the shelf in the quiet room, and I look at it sometimes, and I remember that spring comes twice, if you are lucky, and if you are paying attention, and if you are willing to plant a seed, even when you think it is too late. It is never too late. The seed does not know how old you are. It only knows whether you planted it, and whether you watered it, and whether you waited. Plant it. Water it. Wait. The spring will come."

How to Lose an Argument Well is an essay by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins). Losing an argument gracefully, and what losing well is for.

An original Signature essay, written for this database in 2026.

An argument is for understanding, not for winning; losing well is a skill worth practising. The essay's techniques, listening, repeating back, and asking questions, are practised in the essay itself. As evidence, the essay says: "It is two monologues, delivered simultaneously, to no one."

Full text

I have lost many arguments in my life, and I have won some, and I have learned, slowly, that losing well is a more valuable skill than winning. Winning an argument feels good, in the moment, and it feels like proof that you were right, and that the other person was wrong, and that the world is as it should be. Losing an argument feels bad, in the moment, and it feels like proof of the opposite. But the feeling is not the truth. The truth is that winning and losing are poor measures of what an argument is for, and that an argument, done well, is not a contest at all. It is a conversation, and a conversation has no winner. I learned this the hard way, over many years, by winning arguments and discovering that the winning had cost me more than it was worth. I won an argument with my brother once, about money, and I was right, and I proved I was right, with facts and figures, and he conceded, and we did not speak properly for a year. I won the argument. I lost the brother, for a year, and nearly for longer. I won an argument with a colleague once, about a decision at work, and I was right, and the decision went my way, and the colleague never trusted me again. I won the argument. I lost the trust. I have won many arguments in this way, and each time I have learned, a little more slowly than I should have, that winning is not the point, and that the point is something else entirely. The point of an argument, I have come to believe, is understanding. Not agreement, though agreement is welcome when it comes honestly, but understanding: the understanding of what the other person thinks, and why they think it, and what they care about, and what they are afraid of. An argument is a way of finding out what another person believes, and why, and that finding out is valuable even when, or especially when, you disagree with what you find. I have changed my mind in arguments, and I have been glad to change it, because the changing of it meant that I had learned something, and learning something is better than winning. Losing an argument well begins with listening, which is harder than it sounds. Listening, in an argument, does not mean waiting for your turn to speak. It does not mean rehearsing your reply while the other person is talking. It means actually hearing what they are saying, and trying to understand it, on its own terms, before you decide whether you agree with it. I have been guilty, many times, of the other kind of listening, the kind where I nod and wait, and the moment the other person pauses, I begin my reply, which I had prepared three minutes ago, and which has nothing to do with what they just said. That is not listening. That is performing. And it is the reason most arguments go nowhere. Real listening requires you to set aside, temporarily, your own position, and to enter, imaginatively, into the other person's. It requires you to ask: if I believed what they believe, and I had lived the life they have lived, would I think what they think? The answer is often yes, and the yes is instructive. It does not mean you agree. It means you understand, and understanding is the precondition for any argument that is worth having. An argument between two people who do not understand each other is not an argument. It is two monologues, delivered simultaneously, to no one. Losing well also requires you to admit when you are wrong, which is the hardest part, and the most important. I have been wrong in arguments, many times, and I have known I was wrong, in the moment, and I have kept arguing anyway, because admitting it felt like losing, and losing felt like failure. That is a mistake, and it is a common one. Admitting you are wrong is not losing. It is learning, in public, and learning in public is a brave thing to do, and a rare one. The people I admire most are the people who can say, in the middle of an argument: "You are right. I was wrong. I have changed my mind." I have said it myself, on occasion, and each time I have felt, not smaller, but larger, as if the admission had made room in me for something new. There is a technique to losing well, and I have practised it, imperfectly, for years. When I feel myself becoming angry in an argument, I try to pause, and to breathe, and to ask a question, rather than making a statement. A question is a better tool than a statement, in an argument, because a question invites the other person to say more, and the more they say, the more you understand, and the more you understand, the less angry you become. I have also learned to repeat back what the other person has said, in my own words, and to ask whether I have understood them correctly. This is a simple technique, and it is remarkably effective, because it forces you to listen, and it shows the other person that you have listened, and it often reveals that you had misunderstood them, and that the argument was, in part, an argument about a misunderstanding. I have also learned to choose my arguments. Not every disagreement is worth an argument, and not every argument is worth winning, or losing. Some disagreements are about taste, or about habit, or about things that do not matter, and arguing about them is a waste of time and of goodwill. Some disagreements are about things that matter deeply, and those are worth arguing about, carefully, and with respect, and with the willingness to lose. I have learned to ask, before I argue: does this matter? And if it does not matter, I try, not always successfully, to let it go. Letting go is a form of losing well, and it is the form that requires the most discipline, because it requires you to give up the pleasure of being right, which is a real pleasure, and a seductive one. The best arguments I have had were the ones I lost, or the ones in which no one won, because both people changed their minds a little, and both people left understanding more than they had understood when they began. I had an argument with a friend once, about a political question, on which we disagreed completely, and we argued for two hours, and at the end of it neither of us had changed our position, but both of us understood the other's position better, and we were closer friends for it, not further apart. That is what an argument is for. Not to win. Not to lose. To understand. And to be understood. Losing well also means knowing when to stop. An argument that goes on too long stops being an argument and becomes a siege, and sieges do not end in understanding. They end in exhaustion, and in resentment, and in words that cannot be taken back. I have said words in arguments that I regretted for years, words that were true, in a narrow sense, and cruel, in a wider one, and that did damage no winning could repair. I have learned, slowly, to recognise the moment when an argument has stopped being useful, and to say: let us stop here. Let us come back to this tomorrow. Let us agree that we disagree, and go and do something else. That stopping is not a defeat. It is a mercy, to both people, and it is often the wisest thing you can do in an argument. There is also the matter of the audience, which complicates arguments in ways we do not always admit. Many arguments are not really between two people. They are performances, for the benefit of other people who are watching, and the performance makes losing impossible, because to lose in front of an audience feels like a public humiliation. I have argued badly in front of audiences, and I have watched others do the same, and I have learned that the best arguments happen in private, where no one is watching, and where both people can afford to be wrong, and to admit it, without losing face. If you must argue in public, try to remember that the audience is not the point. The other person is the point. Speak to them, not to the room. I have come to think of losing well as a form of generosity. When you lose well, you are giving the other person something: the acknowledgement that they were right, or that they had a point, or that you understand them, even if you do not agree. That giving costs you nothing, in the long run, and it gives them a great deal. It gives them the feeling of having been heard, which is what most people want, more than they want to win. And it gives you something too: the freedom of not having to be right all the time, which is a heavy burden, and which I am glad to have put down, most of the time. There is one more thing about losing well, and it is the hardest to practise, which is losing well to someone you do not like. It is easy to lose gracefully to a friend, or to someone you respect, because the losing feels like a gift, and the gift feels good to give. It is much harder to lose gracefully to someone you dislike, or to someone who has treated you badly, or to someone whose views you find offensive, because the losing feels like surrender, and the surrender feels like a defeat, and the defeat feels unjust. I have lost arguments to people I did not like, and I have done it badly, with resentment, and with a coldness that I am not proud of, and I have done it well, on occasion, with a grace that surprised me, and the well-losing was always better, not because the other person deserved it, but because I deserved to be the person who could do it. Losing well to someone you dislike is not about them. It is about you, and about the person you want to be, and the person you want to be is someone who can lose well, to anyone, even to someone they do not like. I have also found that losing well is easier when you remember that the argument is not the relationship. The relationship is larger than the argument, and it will outlast it, if you let it, and the argument is only a small part of it, a disagreement about one thing, among many things on which you agree, or on which you have never disagreed. I have had arguments with people I love, about things that mattered deeply, and I have lost those arguments, and the relationship survived, because we both knew that the argument was not the whole of it, and that the love was larger than the disagreement. That knowing is what makes losing well possible, and it is worth cultivating, in every relationship, before the argument begins. If you are in an argument now, or if you are about to be in one, I would suggest this: listen first. Repeat back what you heard. Ask a question. Admit it if you are wrong. Choose the arguments that matter, and let the others go. And if you lose, lose well, with grace, and with curiosity, and with the knowledge that losing well is not a failure. It is a skill, and it is a rare one, and it is worth practising, because the people who can lose well are the people you want to argue with, and the people you want to live with, and the people you want to be.

# Program: full analysis of How to Lose an Argument Well
TEXT = 'I have lost many arguments in my life, and I have won some, and I have learned, slowly, that losing well is a more valuable skill than winning. Winning an argument feels good, in the moment, and it feels like proof that you were right, and that the other person was wrong, and that the world is as it should be. Losing an argument feels bad, in the moment, and it feels like proof of the opposite. But the feeling is not the truth. The truth is that winning and losing are poor measures of what an argument is for, and that an argument, done well, is not a contest at all. It is a conversation, and a conversation has no winner.\nI learned this the hard way, over many years, by winning arguments and discovering that the winning had cost me more than it was worth. I won an argument with my brother once, about money, and I was right, and I proved I was right, with facts and figures, and he conceded, and we did not speak properly for a year. I won the argument. I lost the brother, for a year, and nearly for longer. I won an argument with a colleague once, about a decision at work, and I was right, and the decision went my way, and the colleague never trusted me again. I won the argument. I lost the trust. I have won many arguments in this way, and each time I have learned, a little more slowly than I should have, that winning is not the point, and that the point is something else entirely.\nThe point of an argument, I have come to believe, is understanding. Not agreement, though agreement is welcome when it comes honestly, but understanding: the understanding of what the other person thinks, and why they think it, and what they care about, and what they are afraid of. An argument is a way of finding out what another person believes, and why, and that finding out is valuable even when, or especially when, you disagree with what you find. I have changed my mind in arguments, and I have been glad to change it, because the changing of it meant that I had learned something, and learning something is better than winning.\nLosing an argument well begins with listening, which is harder than it sounds. Listening, in an argument, does not mean waiting for your turn to speak. It does not mean rehearsing your reply while the other person is talking. It means actually hearing what they are saying, and trying to understand it, on its own terms, before you decide whether you agree with it. I have been guilty, many times, of the other kind of listening, the kind where I nod and wait, and the moment the other person pauses, I begin my reply, which I had prepared three minutes ago, and which has nothing to do with what they just said. That is not listening. That is performing. And it is the reason most arguments go nowhere.\nReal listening requires you to set aside, temporarily, your own position, and to enter, imaginatively, into the other person\'s. It requires you to ask: if I believed what they believe, and I had lived the life they have lived, would I think what they think? The answer is often yes, and the yes is instructive. It does not mean you agree. It means you understand, and understanding is the precondition for any argument that is worth having. An argument between two people who do not understand each other is not an argument. It is two monologues, delivered simultaneously, to no one.\nLosing well also requires you to admit when you are wrong, which is the hardest part, and the most important. I have been wrong in arguments, many times, and I have known I was wrong, in the moment, and I have kept arguing anyway, because admitting it felt like losing, and losing felt like failure. That is a mistake, and it is a common one. Admitting you are wrong is not losing. It is learning, in public, and learning in public is a brave thing to do, and a rare one. The people I admire most are the people who can say, in the middle of an argument: "You are right. I was wrong. I have changed my mind." I have said it myself, on occasion, and each time I have felt, not smaller, but larger, as if the admission had made room in me for something new.\nThere is a technique to losing well, and I have practised it, imperfectly, for years. When I feel myself becoming angry in an argument, I try to pause, and to breathe, and to ask a question, rather than making a statement. A question is a better tool than a statement, in an argument, because a question invites the other person to say more, and the more they say, the more you understand, and the more you understand, the less angry you become. I have also learned to repeat back what the other person has said, in my own words, and to ask whether I have understood them correctly. This is a simple technique, and it is remarkably effective, because it forces you to listen, and it shows the other person that you have listened, and it often reveals that you had misunderstood them, and that the argument was, in part, an argument about a misunderstanding.\nI have also learned to choose my arguments. Not every disagreement is worth an argument, and not every argument is worth winning, or losing. Some disagreements are about taste, or about habit, or about things that do not matter, and arguing about them is a waste of time and of goodwill. Some disagreements are about things that matter deeply, and those are worth arguing about, carefully, and with respect, and with the willingness to lose. I have learned to ask, before I argue: does this matter? And if it does not matter, I try, not always successfully, to let it go. Letting go is a form of losing well, and it is the form that requires the most discipline, because it requires you to give up the pleasure of being right, which is a real pleasure, and a seductive one.\nThe best arguments I have had were the ones I lost, or the ones in which no one won, because both people changed their minds a little, and both people left understanding more than they had understood when they began. I had an argument with a friend once, about a political question, on which we disagreed completely, and we argued for two hours, and at the end of it neither of us had changed our position, but both of us understood the other\'s position better, and we were closer friends for it, not further apart. That is what an argument is for. Not to win. Not to lose. To understand. And to be understood.\nLosing well also means knowing when to stop. An argument that goes on too long stops being an argument and becomes a siege, and sieges do not end in understanding. They end in exhaustion, and in resentment, and in words that cannot be taken back. I have said words in arguments that I regretted for years, words that were true, in a narrow sense, and cruel, in a wider one, and that did damage no winning could repair. I have learned, slowly, to recognise the moment when an argument has stopped being useful, and to say: let us stop here. Let us come back to this tomorrow. Let us agree that we disagree, and go and do something else. That stopping is not a defeat. It is a mercy, to both people, and it is often the wisest thing you can do in an argument.\nThere is also the matter of the audience, which complicates arguments in ways we do not always admit. Many arguments are not really between two people. They are performances, for the benefit of other people who are watching, and the performance makes losing impossible, because to lose in front of an audience feels like a public humiliation. I have argued badly in front of audiences, and I have watched others do the same, and I have learned that the best arguments happen in private, where no one is watching, and where both people can afford to be wrong, and to admit it, without losing face. If you must argue in public, try to remember that the audience is not the point. The other person is the point. Speak to them, not to the room.\nI have come to think of losing well as a form of generosity. When you lose well, you are giving the other person something: the acknowledgement that they were right, or that they had a point, or that you understand them, even if you do not agree. That giving costs you nothing, in the long run, and it gives them a great deal. It gives them the feeling of having been heard, which is what most people want, more than they want to win. And it gives you something too: the freedom of not having to be right all the time, which is a heavy burden, and which I am glad to have put down, most of the time.\nThere is one more thing about losing well, and it is the hardest to practise, which is losing well to someone you do not like. It is easy to lose gracefully to a friend, or to someone you respect, because the losing feels like a gift, and the gift feels good to give. It is much harder to lose gracefully to someone you dislike, or to someone who has treated you badly, or to someone whose views you find offensive, because the losing feels like surrender, and the surrender feels like a defeat, and the defeat feels unjust. I have lost arguments to people I did not like, and I have done it badly, with resentment, and with a coldness that I am not proud of, and I have done it well, on occasion, with a grace that surprised me, and the well-losing was always better, not because the other person deserved it, but because I deserved to be the person who could do it. Losing well to someone you dislike is not about them. It is about you, and about the person you want to be, and the person you want to be is someone who can lose well, to anyone, even to someone they do not like.\nI have also found that losing well is easier when you remember that the argument is not the relationship. The relationship is larger than the argument, and it will outlast it, if you let it, and the argument is only a small part of it, a disagreement about one thing, among many things on which you agree, or on which you have never disagreed. I have had arguments with people I love, about things that mattered deeply, and I have lost those arguments, and the relationship survived, because we both knew that the argument was not the whole of it, and that the love was larger than the disagreement. That knowing is what makes losing well possible, and it is worth cultivating, in every relationship, before the argument begins.\nIf you are in an argument now, or if you are about to be in one, I would suggest this: listen first. Repeat back what you heard. Ask a question. Admit it if you are wrong. Choose the arguments that matter, and let the others go. And if you lose, lose well, with grace, and with curiosity, and with the knowledge that losing well is not a failure. It is a skill, and it is a rare one, and it is worth practising, because the people who can lose well are the people you want to argue with, and the people you want to live with, and the people you want to be.'

The Night Train is an essay by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins). The slow night train, and the night it gives you.

An original Signature essay, written for this database in 2026.

The night train is the best way of travelling, because it gives you a night that belongs to the journey. The essay is a journey; it moves through the dark, and arrives in the morning, tired and happy. As evidence, the essay says: "There is a moment on every night train that I have come to watch for, and it is the moment when the train stops, in the middle of nowhere, for no reason that is explained, and the sound of the wheels stops, and the silence is sudden and complete."
