Full text

I have always loved the night train. Not the fast train, which arrives before you have had time to settle, and not the commuter train, which is full of people going to work, but the slow night train, which leaves late in the evening and arrives early in the morning, and which gives you a whole night to travel in. I took the night train many times when I was young, because it was cheap, and because it saved the price of a hotel, and I take it still, when I can, because it is the best way I know of travelling, and because it gives you something that no other form of travel gives you: a night that belongs to the journey. The night train has a particular smell, which is the smell of old upholstery, and of coffee from the buffet car, and of the metal of the rails, and of sleep, or the attempt at sleep, in a compartment full of strangers. It has a particular sound, which is the sound of the wheels on the tracks, a rhythm that changes with the speed, and that becomes, after a while, a kind of lullaby, though not a restful one, because the train never stops moving, and neither does the sound. It has a particular light, which is the light of the corridor, dim and yellow, and the light of the passing stations, which flashes past the window and is gone before you can read the name. I have slept on night trains, or tried to, in every position a human body can assume in a seat that was not designed for sleeping. I have slept sitting up, with my head against the window, and woken with a stiff neck and a line on my forehead from the seam of the seat. I have slept lying across two seats, with my coat as a pillow, and woken to find a stranger sitting opposite me, politely pretending not to notice. I have slept in a couchette, in a compartment with five strangers, in a narrow bunk with a thin blanket, and I have slept well, surprisingly well, because the movement of the train is soothing, and because the strangers, for all their strangeness, are quiet, and because there is something about being in a moving bed that makes sleep come easily. The people on a night train are a particular population, and they are different from the people on a day train. They are quieter. They are more patient. They have accepted that the night will be long, and uncomfortable, and that they will arrive tired, and they have made their peace with it. There are the young people with backpacks, going to a city they have never seen, full of excitement and unable to sleep. There are the old people, travelling to visit family, who sleep the whole way, as if the train were their own bed. There are the workers, coming off a shift, who fall asleep before the train leaves the station. There are the lovers, sitting close together, whispering, and the solitary travellers, like me, who sit by the window and watch the dark go by. The window is the best part of the night train. In the day, the window shows you the landscape, and the landscape is interesting, but it is the landscape you could see from a car, or from a walk. At night, the window shows you something else: the dark, and the lights, and the occasional illuminated thing that flashes past and is gone before you can understand it. A house, lit up, in the middle of nowhere. A factory, with its windows blazing. A station, empty, with a single figure on the platform. A town, spread out below the line, its lights like a map. The night window is a series of glimpses, and the glimpses are more suggestive than any view, because they leave room for the imagination. You do not see the place. You see a light, and you imagine the place, and the imagining is the pleasure. There is a moment on every night train that I have come to watch for, and it is the moment when the train stops, in the middle of nowhere, for no reason that is explained, and the sound of the wheels stops, and the silence is sudden and complete. Everyone wakes up, or stirs, and looks around, and wonders what has happened, and then the train starts again, and the sound resumes, and everyone settles back, and the journey continues. That moment of stillness, in the middle of the night, in the middle of nowhere, is one of the most peaceful moments I know. It is the world, pausing, and then going on, and it reminds me that the journey is not only about arriving. It is about the travelling, and the pauses, and the dark, and the sound of the wheels. The night train arrives in the early morning, usually, and the arrival is always a little disorienting. You wake up, stiff and tired, and the train is slowing down, and the city is coming into view, and you realise that you have travelled all night, and that you are somewhere else, and that the somewhere else is real, and that you are going to spend the day there. There is a particular feeling to arriving by night train, a feeling of having earned the arrival, in a way that arriving by plane does not give you. You did not fly over the land. You travelled through it, in the dark, and the travelling was part of the arriving. There is a particular kind of conversation that happens on a night train, and it is different from any other kind of conversation. It happens late, when the lights are dim, and the train is full of people who are half asleep, and someone says something, to no one in particular, and someone else answers, and a conversation begins, between strangers, that would never begin in daylight. I have had conversations on night trains that I still remember, years later, though I have forgotten the names of the people I had them with, and the places we were going. A man who told me about his daughter, who had just been born, and who showed me a photograph, and who was travelling to see her for the first time. A woman who told me about her mother, who had died, and about the house she was going to clear out, and about the things she expected to find there. A young man who told me about the job he was going to, in a city he had never seen, and about his fear, and his hope, in equal measure. These conversations were brief, and they were honest, in a way that conversations in daylight rarely are, because the night train gives people permission to be honest. It is dark. It is late. You will never see each other again. You can say what you really think, and what you really feel, and the stranger will listen, and will not judge, because they are a stranger, and because they are also travelling through the night. The buffet car, when there is one, is the social centre of the night train, and it is a strange, liminal place, full of people who are neither asleep nor awake, drinking coffee that is too hot, and eating sandwiches that are too expensive, and talking in low voices. I have spent hours in buffet cars, watching the dark go by through the window, and listening to the conversations around me, in languages I did not understand, and in languages I did, and feeling a sense of belonging that I did not expect. The buffet car is a place where everyone is in transit, and where no one belongs, and where, for that reason, everyone belongs together. It is a temporary community, and it dissolves at dawn, when the train arrives, and everyone goes their separate way, and never meets again. That temporariness is part of its charm. It asks nothing of you except that you be there, for a little while, in the night, on the train. I have also learned, on night trains, to appreciate the value of a bad night's sleep, which sounds like a contradiction, and is not. A bad night's sleep on a train is different from a bad night's sleep at home. At home, a bad night's sleep is a failure, and you lie awake resenting it. On a train, a bad night's sleep is part of the journey, and you accept it, and you watch the dark, and you listen to the wheels, and you doze, and you wake, and you doze again, and the night passes, and you arrive, tired, but not unhappy. The tiredness is earned. It is the tiredness of having travelled, and it is a good tiredness, the kind that makes the first coffee of the morning taste better than any coffee you have ever had. The night train has also taught me that the best journeys are the ones you do not plan too carefully. I have taken night trains with a plan, with a timetable and a destination and a list of things to do when I arrived, and I have taken them without a plan, with only a ticket and a bag, and the unplanned journeys were always better. The plan makes you impatient. It makes you watch the clock, and count the hours, and wish the train would go faster. The absence of a plan makes you patient. It lets you enjoy the night for what it is, rather than resenting it for what it is not. I have learned to travel without a plan, when I can, and to let the journey be the point, and to trust that the arrival will take care of itself. There is a sadness to the night train now, and I should mention it, because it is real. The night trains are disappearing. The lines are closing. The services are being withdrawn, because they are not profitable, and because people prefer to fly, and because the world has decided that speed matters more than the journey. I have watched night trains I loved disappear from the timetable, one by one, and I have mourned them, in a small way, because they were part of how I understood travel, and part of how I understood the night. I hope they come back, in some form, because the world is poorer without them, and because there are still people who want to travel slowly, through the dark, and to arrive in the morning, tired and happy, in a city they have never seen. The night train has also shown me that the dark is not empty, which is something I did not know when I was young. When you look out of the window at night, you see darkness, and you think there is nothing there, but there is something there: the fields, and the trees, and the houses, and the rivers, and all the things that are there in the day, only hidden by the dark. The night train teaches you that the world does not stop when you cannot see it, and that it goes on, in the dark, without you, and that you are a guest in it, travelling through, and that the travelling is a privilege. I have looked out of the window at night and thought about the people sleeping in the houses I pass, and about the lives they are living, and about the fact that I will never know them, and that they will never know me, and that the train passes through their lives for a second, and is gone, and that second is enough to remind me how large the world is, and how small I am in it. If you have never taken a night train, take one, before they are gone. Take it slowly. Take it with a book you will not finish, and a sandwich you will eat at midnight, and a window seat, if you can get one. Sleep if you can. Do not sleep if you cannot. Watch the dark go by. Listen to the wheels. Wake up in a new city, in the early morning, tired and happy, and know that you have travelled, really travelled, in the old way, through the night, and that the night, for once, belonged to you.

The night trains are disappearing. The lines are closing. The services are being withdrawn, because they are not profitable, and because people prefer to fly, and because the world has decided that speed matters more than the journey. I have watched night trains I loved disappear from the timetable, one by one, and I have mourned them, in a small way, because they were part of how I understood travel, and part of how I understood the night. I hope they come back, in some form, because the world is poorer without them, and because there are still people who want to travel slowly, through the dark, and to arrive in the morning, tired and happy, in a city they have never seen. The night train has also shown me that the dark is not empty, which is something I did not know when I was young. When you look out of the window at night, you see darkness, and you think there is nothing there, but there is something there: the fields, and the trees, and the houses, and the rivers, and all the things that are there in the day, only hidden by the dark. The night train teaches you that the world does not stop when you cannot see it, and that it goes on, in the dark, without you, and that you are a guest in it, travelling through, and that the travelling is a privilege. I have looked out of the window at night and thought about the people sleeping in the houses I pass, and about the lives they are living, and about the fact that I will never know them, and that they will never know me, and that the train passes through their lives for a second, and is gone, and that second is enough to remind me how large the world is, and how small I am in it.\nIf you have never taken a night train, take one, before they are gone. Take it slowly. Take it with a book you will not finish, and a sandwich you will eat at midnight, and a window seat, if you can get one. Sleep if you can. Do not sleep if you cannot. Watch the dark go by. Listen to the wheels. Wake up in a new city, in the early morning, tired and happy, and know that you have travelled, really travelled, in the old way, through the night, and that the night, for once, belonged to you."
words = TEXT.split()

# Program: full analysis of The Night Train
TEXT = "I have always loved the night train. Not the fast train, which arrives before you have had time to settle, and not the commuter train, which is full of people going to work, but the slow night train, which leaves late in the evening and arrives early in the morning, and which gives you a whole night to travel in. I took the night train many times when I was young, because it was cheap, and because it saved the price of a hotel, and I take it still, when I can, because it is the best way I know of travelling, and because it gives you something that no other form of travel gives you: a night that belongs to the journey.\nThe night train has a particular smell, which is the smell of old upholstery, and of coffee from the buffet car, and of the metal of the rails, and of sleep, or the attempt at sleep, in a compartment full of strangers. It has a particular sound, which is the sound of the wheels on the tracks, a rhythm that changes with the speed, and that becomes, after a while, a kind of lullaby, though not a restful one, because the train never stops moving, and neither does the sound. It has a particular light, which is the light of the corridor, dim and yellow, and the light of the passing stations, which flashes past the window and is gone before you can read the name.\nI have slept on night trains, or tried to, in every position a human body can assume in a seat that was not designed for sleeping. I have slept sitting up, with my head against the window, and woken with a stiff neck and a line on my forehead from the seam of the seat. I have slept lying across two seats, with my coat as a pillow, and woken to find a stranger sitting opposite me, politely pretending not to notice. I have slept in a couchette, in a compartment with five strangers, in a narrow bunk with a thin blanket, and I have slept well, surprisingly well, because the movement of the train is soothing, and because the strangers, for all their strangeness, are quiet, and because there is something about being in a moving bed that makes sleep come easily.\nThe people on a night train are a particular population, and they are different from the people on a day train. They are quieter. They are more patient. They have accepted that the night will be long, and uncomfortable, and that they will arrive tired, and they have made their peace with it. There are the young people with backpacks, going to a city they have never seen, full of excitement and unable to sleep. There are the old people, travelling to visit family, who sleep the whole way, as if the train were their own bed. There are the workers, coming off a shift, who fall asleep before the train leaves the station. There are the lovers, sitting close together, whispering, and the solitary travellers, like me, who sit by the window and watch the dark go by.\nThe window is the best part of the night train. In the day, the window shows you the landscape, and the landscape is interesting, but it is the landscape you could see from a car, or from a walk. At night, the window shows you something else: the dark, and the lights, and the occasional illuminated thing that flashes past and is gone before you can understand it. A house, lit up, in the middle of nowhere. A factory, with its windows blazing. A station, empty, with a single figure on the platform. A town, spread out below the line, its lights like a map. The night window is a series of glimpses, and the glimpses are more suggestive than any view, because they leave room for the imagination. You do not see the place. You see a light, and you imagine the place, and the imagining is the pleasure.\nThere is a moment on every night train that I have come to watch for, and it is the moment when the train stops, in the middle of nowhere, for no reason that is explained, and the sound of the wheels stops, and the silence is sudden and complete. Everyone wakes up, or stirs, and looks around, and wonders what has happened, and then the train starts again, and the sound resumes, and everyone settles back, and the journey continues. That moment of stillness, in the middle of the night, in the middle of nowhere, is one of the most peaceful moments I know. It is the world, pausing, and then going on, and it reminds me that the journey is not only about arriving. It is about the travelling, and the pauses, and the dark, and the sound of the wheels.\nThe night train arrives in the early morning, usually, and the arrival is always a little disorienting. You wake up, stiff and tired, and the train is slowing down, and the city is coming into view, and you realise that you have travelled all night, and that you are somewhere else, and that the somewhere else is real, and that you are going to spend the day there. There is a particular feeling to arriving by night train, a feeling of having earned the arrival, in a way that arriving by plane does not give you. You did not fly over the land. You travelled through it, in the dark, and the travelling was part of the arriving.\nThere is a particular kind of conversation that happens on a night train, and it is different from any other kind of conversation. It happens late, when the lights are dim, and the train is full of people who are half asleep, and someone says something, to no one in particular, and someone else answers, and a conversation begins, between strangers, that would never begin in daylight. I have had conversations on night trains that I still remember, years later, though I have forgotten the names of the people I had them with, and the places we were going. A man who told me about his daughter, who had just been born, and who showed me a photograph, and who was travelling to see her for the first time. A woman who told me about her mother, who had died, and about the house she was going to clear out, and about the things she expected to find there. A young man who told me about the job he was going to, in a city he had never seen, and about his fear, and his hope, in equal measure. These conversations were brief, and they were honest, in a way that conversations in daylight rarely are, because the night train gives people permission to be honest. It is dark. It is late. You will never see each other again. You can say what you really think, and what you really feel, and the stranger will listen, and will not judge, because they are a stranger, and because they are also travelling through the night.\nThe buffet car, when there is one, is the social centre of the night train, and it is a strange, liminal place, full of people who are neither asleep nor awake, drinking coffee that is too hot, and eating sandwiches that are too expensive, and talking in low voices. I have spent hours in buffet cars, watching the dark go by through the window, and listening to the conversations around me, in languages I did not understand, and in languages I did, and feeling a sense of belonging that I did not expect. The buffet car is a place where everyone is in transit, and where no one belongs, and where, for that reason, everyone belongs together. It is a temporary community, and it dissolves at dawn, when the train arrives, and everyone goes their separate way, and never meets again. That temporariness is part of its charm. It asks nothing of you except that you be there, for a little while, in the night, on the train.\nI have also learned, on night trains, to appreciate the value of a bad night's sleep, which sounds like a contradiction, and is not. A bad night's sleep on a train is different from a bad night's sleep at home. At home, a bad night's sleep is a failure, and you lie awake resenting it. On a train, a bad night's sleep is part of the journey, and you accept it, and you watch the dark, and you listen to the wheels, and you doze, and you wake, and you doze again, and the night passes, and you arrive, tired, but not unhappy. The tiredness is earned. It is the tiredness of having travelled, and it is a good tiredness, the kind that makes the first coffee of the morning taste better than any coffee you have ever had.\nThe night train has also taught me that the best journeys are the ones you do not plan too carefully. I have taken night trains with a plan, with a timetable and a destination and a list of things to do when I arrived, and I have taken them without a plan, with only a ticket and a bag, and the unplanned journeys were always better. The plan makes you impatient. It makes you watch the clock, and count the hours, and wish the train would go faster. The absence of a plan makes you patient. It lets you enjoy the night for what it is, rather than resenting it for what it is not. I have learned to travel without a plan, when I can, and to let the journey be the point, and to trust that the arrival will take care of itself.\nThere is a sadness to the night train now, and I should mention it, because it is real. The night trains are disappearing. The lines are closing. The services are being withdrawn, because they are not profitable, and because people prefer to fly, and because the world has decided that speed matters more than the journey. I have watched night trains I loved disappear from the timetable, one by one, and I have mourned them, in a small way, because they were part of how I understood travel, and part of how I understood the night. I hope they come back, in some form, because the world is poorer without them, and because there are still people who want to travel slowly, through the dark, and to arrive in the morning, tired and happy, in a city they have never seen. The night train has also shown me that the dark is not empty, which is something I did not know when I was young. When you look out of the window at night, you see darkness, and you think there is nothing there, but there is something there: the fields, and the trees, and the houses, and the rivers, and all the things that are there in the day, only hidden by the dark. The night train teaches you that the world does not stop when you cannot see it, and that it goes on, in the dark, without you, and that you are a guest in it, travelling through, and that the travelling is a privilege. I have looked out of the window at night and thought about the people sleeping in the houses I pass, and about the lives they are living, and about the fact that I will never know them, and that they will never know me, and that the train passes through their lives for a second, and is gone, and that second is enough to remind me how large the world is, and how small I am in it.\nIf you have never taken a night train, take one, before they are gone. Take it slowly. Take it with a book you will not finish, and a sandwich you will eat at midnight, and a window seat, if you can get one. Sleep if you can. Do not sleep if you cannot. Watch the dark go by. Listen to the wheels. Wake up in a new city, in the early morning, tired and happy, and know that you have travelled, really travelled, in the old way, through the night, and that the night, for once, belonged to you."

The Work of Remembering is an essay by JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins). Remembering as work, and the duty of it.

An original Signature essay, written for this database in 2026.

Remembering is work, and it is the most important work there is; it is how love continues after a person is gone. The essay's record, notebook, stories, and places, is its method and its message. As evidence, the essay says: "A mind that remembered everything would be uninhabitable."

Full text

Remembering is work. I did not understand this when I was young, because when you are young, remembering is easy. You remember what happened yesterday, and last week, and last year, without trying, because the past is close, and the details are fresh, and the memories come when you call them. As you grow older, remembering becomes harder, and it becomes work, in the same way that walking becomes work, and seeing becomes work, and all the things that were once easy become things you must do deliberately, with effort, and with care. I first noticed this in my fifties, when I began to forget the names of people I had known for years, and to forget why I had walked into a room, and to forget the word I wanted, in the middle of a sentence, and to stand there, searching for it, while the person I was talking to waited politely. These were small forgettings, and they were, I told myself, normal, and they were normal, but they were also a warning. The memory, like the body, needs exercise, and I had not been exercising it. I had been relying on it, without maintaining it, and it was beginning to show the strain. I began to do the work of remembering deliberately, and the first thing I did was to keep a notebook, which I have written about elsewhere, and which is the single most useful tool for remembering that I know. The notebook does not remember for you. It records, and the recording is a form of remembering, and the reading back of it is another form, and together they keep the memories alive. But the notebook is not enough, on its own. It is a tool, and like any tool it must be used, and the using of it is work. The second thing I did was to tell stories. I began to tell my children, and then my grandchildren, the stories of my life: where I grew up, what my parents were like, what the town looked like when I was a boy, what I did, what I got wrong, what I learned. At first I told the stories because the children asked, and then I told them because I wanted to, and then I told them because I realised that the telling was itself a form of remembering. A memory that is never told fades. A memory that is told, again and again, to different people, at different times, stays. The telling fixes it, the way the saying of a poem fixes it. I have told some stories so many times that I know them by heart, and the knowing by heart is a kind of preservation. The third thing I did was to go back to places. I went back to the street where I grew up, and to the school I went to, and to the river I played by, and I walked in those places, and I looked at what had changed, and what had not, and the walking and the looking brought back memories that I had forgotten I had. A place is a powerful trigger for memory. The smell of a street, the shape of a building, the sound of a river: these things unlock memories that no amount of sitting and thinking can reach. I have remembered things, standing in a place I had not visited in forty years, that I could not have remembered at home, and the remembering felt like a gift, as if the place had been keeping the memory for me, and was returning it now that I had come back to collect it. Remembering is also, I have learned, a selective act, and the selection matters. We do not remember everything. We remember what we pay attention to, and what we tell, and what we return to, and the rest fades. That fading is not a failure. It is a necessity. A mind that remembered everything would be uninhabitable. But the selection is a choice, even when it does not feel like one, and the choice shapes the story we tell about our lives. I have chosen, deliberately, to remember the good things, and the kind things, and the funny things, and to let the bitter things fade, not because the bitter things did not happen, but because I do not want them to be the story. That is a choice, and it is mine to make, and I make it every day, in the small acts of remembering and forgetting that make up a life. There is a sadness to the work of remembering, and I should mention it, because it is real. Remembering means remembering people who are gone, and places that have changed, and times that will not come back, and the remembering of them hurts, sometimes, in a way that is difficult to bear. I have sat in the quiet room, remembering my father, and my mother, and friends who have died, and I have felt the loss freshly, as if it had happened yesterday, and I have wondered whether the remembering is worth the pain. It is worth it. The pain is the price of the love, and the love is the reason for the remembering, and the remembering is the way the love continues, after the person is gone. To forget them would be a second loss, and I will not do it. The work of remembering is also the work of making sense. A life, lived day by day, does not make sense while you are living it. It is a series of events, some of them connected, most of them not, and the connections only become visible later, when you look back, and remember, and see the pattern. I have looked back at my life, many times, and I have seen patterns I did not see when I was living it: the decisions that led to other decisions, the accidents that turned out to be turning points, the people who changed everything by being in the right place at the right time. Those patterns are not the whole truth. They are the truth as I remember it, which is the only truth I have. But they are a truth, and they give the life a shape, and the shape is comforting, even when the life itself was messy. Remembering is also a duty, and I mean that in the best sense. We owe it to the people who came before us to remember them, and we owe it to the people who come after us to leave a record. A family that does not remember its past is a family without a foundation, and a person who does not remember their past is a person without a story. I have taken that duty seriously, in recent years, in a way I did not when I was young. I have written down the names of my grandparents, and where they came from, and what they did, and I have told my grandchildren those names, and I have asked them to remember them, not because the names themselves matter, but because the remembering of them is a way of belonging, to a family, and to a history, that is larger than any one person. There is a particular kind of remembering that I practise now, and it is the remembering of ordinary days. Not the great days, the weddings and the births and the achievements, which are easy to remember, but the ordinary days: the Tuesday when nothing happened, the afternoon in the garden, the meal that was nothing special, the walk that went nowhere. Those days are the substance of a life, and they are the days that fade first, because they do not seem important enough to remember. But they are important, because they are the days that made up the life, and a life remembered only in its great moments is a life misremembered. I try, in my notebook, to record the ordinary days, as well as the great ones, and the recording of them is a way of honouring them, and of saying: this day mattered, because I lived it, and because I was here. The work of remembering has also taught me to be careful about what I choose to forget. Forgetting is not always a failure. Sometimes it is a mercy, and sometimes it is a choice, and sometimes it is the only way to go on living. I have forgotten, deliberately, some of the things that hurt me most, not because they did not matter, but because carrying them was heavier than the remembering was worth. I do not think of that forgetting as a betrayal. I think of it as an act of self-preservation, and as a recognition that the mind, like the house, has only so much room, and that what you keep in it should be chosen with care. The memories I have kept are the ones I want to live with. The ones I have let go were the ones that made living harder, and I do not regret letting them go. I have also learned that remembering together is different from remembering alone. When I remember with my wife, or with my children, or with an old friend, the memory becomes something shared, and the sharing changes it. We remember different details, and we correct each other, and we laugh at the corrections, and the memory becomes richer than any one person's version of it. A memory held by two people is stronger than a memory held by one, because it has been tested, and confirmed, and added to, and it belongs to both of them. I have had conversations with my brother about our childhood that lasted for hours, and in which we each remembered things the other had forgotten, and by the end of the conversation we had rebuilt a whole summer, between us, that neither of us could have rebuilt alone. That rebuilding is one of the great pleasures of remembering, and it is only possible with other people. There is a final thing I would say about remembering, and it is this: remember the people who will not be remembered by anyone else. There are people in every family, and in every life, who lived quietly, and who did not leave a mark on the world, and who will be forgotten when the people who knew them are gone. Remember them. Write down their names. Tell their stories, even if the stories are small. A life does not have to be grand to be worth remembering. It only has to have been lived, and to have mattered to someone, and to be remembered by someone, and that someone can be you. I remember my grandmother, who lived a quiet life, and who never did anything that would be written in a history book, and who mattered to me more than almost anyone, and I will go on remembering her, and telling her story, for as long as I can, because that is what remembering is for. Remembering is also a way of saying thank you, and I mean that literally. When I remember someone who helped me, and I tell their story, and I say their name, I am thanking them, in the only way I can, now that they are gone. The remembering is the thanks. I remember the teacher who believed in me, and the friend who lent me money when I had none, and the neighbour who watched my children, and the stranger who helped me when I was lost, and I remember them, and I tell their stories, and the telling is my way of saying: thank you, I have not forgotten, and I will not forget, for as long as I am able to remember. I do not know how much longer I will remember clearly. The memory will fade, as it does for all of us, and the work will become harder, and then, one day, it will become impossible. I accept that. But while I can still do the work, I will do it, because it is the most important work I have. I will keep the notebook. I will tell the stories. I will go back to the places. I will remember, as well as I can, for as long as I can, and I will leave the record behind me, for the people who come after, so that they will know who I was, and what I did, and what I loved, and what I learned. That is the work of remembering, and it is work worth doing.

But they are a truth, and they give the life a shape, and the shape is comforting, even when the life itself was messy.\nRemembering is also a duty, and I mean that in the best sense. We owe it to the people who came before us to remember them, and we owe it to the people who come after us to leave a record. A family that does not remember its past is a family without a foundation, and a person who does not remember their past is a person without a story. I have taken that duty seriously, in recent years, in a way I did not when I was young. I have written down the names of my grandparents, and where they came from, and what they did, and I have told my grandchildren those names, and I have asked them to remember them, not because the names themselves matter, but because the remembering of them is a way of belonging, to a family, and to a history, that is larger than any one person.\nThere is a particular kind of remembering that I practise now, and it is the remembering of ordinary days. Not the great days, the weddings and the births and the achievements, which are easy to remember, but the ordinary days: the Tuesday when nothing happened, the afternoon in the garden, the meal that was nothing special, the walk that went nowhere. Those days are the substance of a life, and they are the days that fade first, because they do not seem important enough to remember. But they are important, because they are the days that made up the life, and a life remembered only in its great moments is a life misremembered. I try, in my notebook, to record the ordinary days, as well as the great ones, and the recording of them is a way of honouring them, and of saying: this day mattered, because I lived it, and because I was here.\nThe work of remembering has also taught me to be careful about what I choose to forget. Forgetting is not always a failure. Sometimes it is a mercy, and sometimes it is a choice, and sometimes it is the only way to go on living. I have forgotten, deliberately, some of the things that hurt me most, not because they did not matter, but because carrying them was heavier than the remembering was worth. I do not think of that forgetting as a betrayal. I think of it as an act of self-preservation, and as a recognition that the mind, like the house, has only so much room, and that what you keep in it should be chosen with care. The memories I have kept are the ones I want to live with. The ones I have let go were the ones that made living harder, and I do not regret letting them go.\nI have also learned that remembering together is different from remembering alone. When I remember with my wife, or with my children, or with an old friend, the memory becomes something shared, and the sharing changes it. We remember different details, and we correct each other, and we laugh at the corrections, and the memory becomes richer than any one person's version of it. A memory held by two people is stronger than a memory held by one, because it has been tested, and confirmed, and added to, and it belongs to both of them. I have had conversations with my brother about our childhood that lasted for hours, and in which we each remembered things the other had forgotten, and by the end of the conversation we had rebuilt a whole summer, between us, that neither of us could have rebuilt alone. That rebuilding is one of the great pleasures of remembering, and it is only possible with other people.\nThere is a final thing I would say about remembering, and it is this: remember the people who will not be remembered by anyone else. There are people in every family, and in every life, who lived quietly, and who did not leave a mark on the world, and who will be forgotten when the people who knew them are gone. Remember them. Write down their names. Tell their stories, even if the stories are small. A life does not have to be grand to be worth remembering. It only has to have been lived, and to have mattered to someone, and to be remembered by someone, and that someone can be you. I remember my grandmother, who lived a quiet life, and who never did anything that would be written in a history book, and who mattered to me more than almost anyone, and I will go on remembering her, and telling her story, for as long as I can, because that is what remembering is for.\nRemembering is also a way of saying thank you, and I mean that literally. When I remember someone who helped me, and I tell their story, and I say their name, I am thanking them, in the only way I can, now that they are gone. The remembering is the thanks. I remember the teacher who believed in me, and the friend who lent me money when I had none, and the neighbour who watched my children, and the stranger who helped me when I was lost, and I remember them, and I tell their stories, and the telling is my way of saying: thank you, I have not forgotten, and I will not forget, for as long as I am able to remember.\nI do not know how much longer I will remember clearly. The memory will fade, as it does for all of us, and the work will become harder, and then, one day, it will become impossible. I accept that. But while I can still do the work, I will do it, because it is the most important work I have. I will keep the notebook. I will tell the stories. I will go back to the places. I will remember, as well as I can, for as long as I can, and I will leave the record behind me, for the people who come after, so that they will know who I was, and what I did, and what I loved, and what I learned. That is the work of remembering, and it is work worth doing."
words = TEXT.split()
sentences = [s for s in TEXT.replace(chr(10), ' ').split('.') if s.strip()]

# Program: full analysis of The Work of Remembering
TEXT = "Remembering is work. I did not understand this when I was young, because when you are young, remembering is easy. You remember what happened yesterday, and last week, and last year, without trying, because the past is close, and the details are fresh, and the memories come when you call them. As you grow older, remembering becomes harder, and it becomes work, in the same way that walking becomes work, and seeing becomes work, and all the things that were once easy become things you must do deliberately, with effort, and with care.\nI first noticed this in my fifties, when I began to forget the names of people I had known for years, and to forget why I had walked into a room, and to forget the word I wanted, in the middle of a sentence, and to stand there, searching for it, while the person I was talking to waited politely. These were small forgettings, and they were, I told myself, normal, and they were normal, but they were also a warning. The memory, like the body, needs exercise, and I had not been exercising it. I had been relying on it, without maintaining it, and it was beginning to show the strain.\nI began to do the work of remembering deliberately, and the first thing I did was to keep a notebook, which I have written about elsewhere, and which is the single most useful tool for remembering that I know. The notebook does not remember for you. It records, and the recording is a form of remembering, and the reading back of it is another form, and together they keep the memories alive. But the notebook is not enough, on its own. It is a tool, and like any tool it must be used, and the using of it is work.\nThe second thing I did was to tell stories. I began to tell my children, and then my grandchildren, the stories of my life: where I grew up, what my parents were like, what the town looked like when I was a boy, what I did, what I got wrong, what I learned. At first I told the stories because the children asked, and then I told them because I wanted to, and then I told them because I realised that the telling was itself a form of remembering. A memory that is never told fades. A memory that is told, again and again, to different people, at different times, stays. The telling fixes it, the way the saying of a poem fixes it. I have told some stories so many times that I know them by heart, and the knowing by heart is a kind of preservation.\nThe third thing I did was to go back to places. I went back to the street where I grew up, and to the school I went to, and to the river I played by, and I walked in those places, and I looked at what had changed, and what had not, and the walking and the looking brought back memories that I had forgotten I had. A place is a powerful trigger for memory. The smell of a street, the shape of a building, the sound of a river: these things unlock memories that no amount of sitting and thinking can reach. I have remembered things, standing in a place I had not visited in forty years, that I could not have remembered at home, and the remembering felt like a gift, as if the place had been keeping the memory for me, and was returning it now that I had come back to collect it.\nRemembering is also, I have learned, a selective act, and the selection matters. We do not remember everything. We remember what we pay attention to, and what we tell, and what we return to, and the rest fades. That fading is not a failure. It is a necessity. A mind that remembered everything would be uninhabitable. But the selection is a choice, even when it does not feel like one, and the choice shapes the story we tell about our lives. I have chosen, deliberately, to remember the good things, and the kind things, and the funny things, and to let the bitter things fade, not because the bitter things did not happen, but because I do not want them to be the story. That is a choice, and it is mine to make, and I make it every day, in the small acts of remembering and forgetting that make up a life.\nThere is a sadness to the work of remembering, and I should mention it, because it is real. Remembering means remembering people who are gone, and places that have changed, and times that will not come back, and the remembering of them hurts, sometimes, in a way that is difficult to bear. I have sat in the quiet room, remembering my father, and my mother, and friends who have died, and I have felt the loss freshly, as if it had happened yesterday, and I have wondered whether the remembering is worth the pain. It is worth it. The pain is the price of the love, and the love is the reason for the remembering, and the remembering is the way the love continues, after the person is gone. To forget them would be a second loss, and I will not do it.\nThe work of remembering is also the work of making sense. A life, lived day by day, does not make sense while you are living it. It is a series of events, some of them connected, most of them not, and the connections only become visible later, when you look back, and remember, and see the pattern. I have looked back at my life, many times, and I have seen patterns I did not see when I was living it: the decisions that led to other decisions, the accidents that turned out to be turning points, the people who changed everything by being in the right place at the right time. Those patterns are not the whole truth. They are the truth as I remember it, which is the only truth I have. But they are a truth, and they give the life a shape, and the shape is comforting, even when the life itself was messy.\nRemembering is also a duty, and I mean that in the best sense.
